#166. A new approach to a Covid-19 nasal vaccine shows early promise

Scientists in Germany say they’ve been able to make a nasal vaccine that can shut down a Covid-19 infection in the nose and throat, where the virus gets its first foothold in the body.

In experiments in hamsters, two doses of the vaccine – which is made with a live but weakened form of the coronavirus that causes Covid-19 – blocked the virus from copying itself in the animals’ upper airways, achieving “sterilizing immunity” and preventing illness, a long-sought goal of the pandemic.

WHO experts revise Covid-19 vaccine advice, say healthy kids and teens low risk

Although this vaccine has several more hurdles to clear before it gets to a doctor’s office or drug store, other nasal vaccines are in use or are nearing the finish line in clinical trials.

China and India both rolled out vaccines given through the nasal tissues last fall, though it’s not clear how well they may be working. Studies on the effectiveness of these vaccines have yet to be published, leaving much of the world to wonder whether this approach to protection really works in people.

The US has reached something of a stalemate with Covid-19. Even with the darkest days of the pandemic behind us, hundreds of Americans are still dying daily as the infection continues to simmer in the background of our return to normal life.

As long as the virus continues to spread among people and animals, there’s always the potential for it mutate into a more contagious or more damaging version of itself. And while Covid infections have become manageable for most healthy people, they may still pose a danger to vulnerable groups such as the elderly and immunocompromised.

Researchers hope next-generation Covid-19 vaccines, which aim to shut down the virus before it ever gets a chance to make us sick and ultimately prevent the spread of infection, could make our newest resident respiratory infection less of a threat.

China approves its first mRNA Covid-19 vaccine

One way scientists are trying to do that is by boosting mucosal immunity, beefing up immune defenses in the tissues that line the upper airways, right where the virus would land and begin to infect our cells.

It’s a bit like stationing firefighters underneath the smoke alarm in your house, says study author Emanuel Wyler, a scientist at the Max Delbruck Center for Molecular Medicine in the Helmholtz Association in Berlin.

The immunity that’s created by shots works throughout the body, but it resides primarily in the blood. That means it may take longer to mount a response.

“If they are already on site, they can immediately eliminate the fire, but if they’re like 2 miles away, they first need to drive there, and by that time, one-third of the house is already in full flames,” Wyler said.

Mucosal vaccines are also better at priming a different kind of first responder than injections do. They do a better job of summoning IgA antibodies, which have four arms to grab onto invaders instead of the two arms that the y-shaped IgG antibodies have. Some scientists think IgA antibodies may be less picky about their targets than IgG antibodies, which makes them better equipped to deal with new variants.

The new nasal vaccine takes a new approach to a very old idea: weakening a virus so it’s no longer a threat and then giving it to people so their immune systems can learn to recognize and fight it off. The first vaccines using this approach date to the 1870s, against anthrax and rabies. Back then, scientists weakened the agents they were using with heat and chemicals.

The researchers manipulated the genetic material in the virus to make it harder for cells to translate. This technique, called codon pair deoptimization, hobbles the virus so it can be shown to the immune system without making the body sick.

“You could imagine reading a text … and every letter is a different font, or every letter is a different size, then the text is much harder to read. And this is basically what we do in codon pair deoptimization,” Wyler said.

In the hamster studies, which were published Monday in the journal Nature Microbiology, two doses of the live but weakened nasal vaccine created a much stronger immune response than either two doses of an mRNA-based vaccine or one that uses an adenovirus to ferry the vaccine instructions into cells.

The researchers think the live weakened vaccine probably worked better because it closely mimics the process of a natural infection.

The nasal vaccine also previews the entire coronavirus for the body, not just its spike proteins like current Covid-19 vaccines do, so the hamsters were able to make immune weapons against a wider range of targets.

As promising as all this sounds, vaccine experts say caution is warranted. This vaccine still has to pass more tests before it’s ready for use, but they say the results look encouraging.

Health officials consider another round of bivalent boosters for the most vulnerable Americans

“They did a very nice job. This is obviously a competent and thoughtful team that did this work, and impressive in the scope of what they did. Now it just needs to be repeated,” perhaps in primates and certainly in humans before it can be widely used, said Dr. Greg Poland, who designs vaccines at the Mayo Clinic. He was not involved in the new research.

The study began in 2021, before the Omicron variant was around, so the vaccine tested in these experiments was made with the original strain of the coronavirus. In the experiments, when they infected animals with Omicron, the live but weakened nasal vaccine still performed better than the others, but its ability to neutralize the virus was diminished. Researchers think it will need an update.

It also needs to be tested in humans, and Wyler says they’re working on that. The scientists have partnered with a Swiss company called RocketVax to start phase I clinical trials.

Other vaccines are further along, but the progress has been “slow and halting,” Poland said. Groups working on these vaccines are struggling to raise the steep costs of getting a new vaccine to market, and they’re doing it in a setting where people tend to think the vaccine race has been won and done.

In reality, Poland said, we’re far from that. All it would take is another Omicron-level shift in the evolution of the virus, and we could be back at square one, with no effective tools against the coronavirus.

“That’s foolish. We should be developing a pan-coronavirus vaccine that does induce mucosal immunity and that is long-lived,” he said.

At least four nasal vaccines for Covid-19 have reached late-stage testing in people, according to the World Health Organization’s vaccine tracker.

The nasal vaccines in use in China and India rely on harmless adenoviruses to ferry their instructions into cells, although effectiveness data for these has not been published.

Two other nasal vaccines are finishing human studies.

Covid vaccine maker Novavax says it may not survive

One, a recombinant vaccine that can be produced cheaply in chicken eggs, the same way many flu vaccines are, is being put through its paces by researchers at Mount Sinai in New York City.

Another, like the German vaccine, uses a live but weakened version of the virus. It’s being developed by a company called Codagenix. Results of those studies, which were carried out in South America and Africa, may come later this year.

The German team says it’s eagerly watching for the Codagenix data.

“They will be very important in order to know where whether this kind of attempt is basically promising or not,” Wyler said.

They have reason to worry. Respiratory infections have proved to be tough targets for inhaled vaccines.

FluMist, a live but weakened form of the flu virus, works reasonably well in children but doesn’t help adults as much. The reason is thought to be that adults already have immune memory for the flu, and when the virus is injected into the nose, the vaccine mostly boosts what’s already there.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

Still, some of the most potent vaccines such as the vaccine against measles, mumps and rubella use live attenuated viruses, so it’s a promising approach.

Another consideration is that live vaccines can’t be taken by everyone. People with very compromised immunity are often cautioned against using live vaccines because even these very weakened viruses may be risky for them.

“Although it’s strongly attenuated, it’s still a real virus,” Wyler said, so it would have to be used carefully.

#164. ‘A source of inspiration’: series of public events celebrate life of Dolores Huerta

A public vigil and celebration of life honor the civil rights activist and co-founder of United Farm Workers union

Hundreds honored and celebrated the life and legacy of California civil rights activist Dolores Huerta this week with a series of public events including a vigil and celebration of life in Bakersfield.

Huerta, a feminist icon who co-founded the United Farm Workers (UFW) in 1962, died on 28 October aged 96.

“Our family has been deeply touched by the condolences, remembrances, and tributes we’ve received from people whose lives our mother, Dolores, impacted,” said Huerta’s family in a statement on Thursday afternoon. “Countless people have shared how she changed the course of their lives, and each story has reminded us of how fully she committed her life to helping and supporting others.”

The week was filled with events dedicated to Huerta’s memory, her courage, resilience and her impact on the community.

On Monday, Huerta was honored in Sacramento by state government officials including Monique Limón, the senate president pro tempore, Robert Rivas, the assembly speaker, Xavier Becerra, the California governor-elect, and community leaders and the public who paid their respects to the world-renowned labor rights leader and activists in a historic ceremony.

Huerta became the first female, first Latino and first non-elected leader to lie in honor at the state capitol. Her casket was escorted in after a march organized by local unions.

“Our mother devoted her entire life to fighting injustice and lifting up communities too often left out and treated unfairly,” said Huerta’s family. “As a lifelong organizer, her activism has been a source of inspiration and empowerment for people around the world.”

For more than six decades, Huerta spearheaded historic boycotts, fought tirelessly for farmworkers’ rights, women’s equality and environmental justice, and coined the enduring national rally cry, “¡Sí, se puede!”

Huerta has received numerous honors including the Eleanor Roosevelt Human Rights Awards. In 2012, Barack Obama awarded Huerta the Presidential Medal of Freedom, the nation’s highest civilian honor.

A two-day tribute started on Thursday evening with a public rosary and vigil in Bakersfield, where she had lived since 1990. The public was invited to pay their respects after the rosary.

On Friday, an interfaith memorial service and celebration of life will begin with an opening blessing by Aztec dancers followed by the memorial service and the celebration of life program. Music groups Los Lobos, Ozomatli, Las Cafeteras, Nancy Sanchez and Trio los Dados are expected to perform. The event will also feature speakers including Becerra, Rob Bonta, the California attorney general, Teresa Romero, the UFW president, actor Eva Longoria, and Cristela Alonzo, a comedian, among others.

Both public events will be live streamed on the Dolores Huerta Foundation YouTube channel.

Her legacy will continue through the work and empowering mission of the Dolores Huerta Foundation. In 2003, Huerta founded the grassroots community organization to create leadership opportunities for community members, while organizing underserved communities, and pursuing social justice.

Huerta kept on working even as a nonagenarian, dedicating her life as an organizer.

In the Central valley, Huerta supported minorities to run for office to make a difference at the local, state and federal level. In 2022, Huerta spoke at a USCIS Special Children’s Ceremony in Delano, encouraging new US citizens to fulfill their civic responsibilities, especially voting.

For her 94th birthday, Huerta asked people to help her celebrate her birthday by getting others involved in elections either by talking to somebody about voting and about registering to vote. She encouraged legal residents to become US citizens to exercise their rights to vote.

Huerta urged uninsured Latinos and Californians to enroll in health coverage with the passage of the Affordable Care Act partnering with Covered California to spread the message. Huerta also had several schools named after her throughout the state. Last year, Huerta joined Central valley educators to demand David Valadao, a US representative from California, to defend funding for schools.

#163. The $500 billion beauty industry's 'green' ambitions are a patchwork at best. And they're falling short

The escalating climate crisis is shifting many people’s purchasing patterns and this extends to the $500 billion dollar global beauty industry which is grappling with a range of sustainability challenges across product manufacturing, packaging and disposal.

'Delay means death': We're running out of ways to adapt to the climate crisis, new report shows. Here are the key takeaways

Strategy and consulting firm Simon Kucher’s Global Sustainability Study 2021 found 60% of consumers around the world rated sustainability as an important purchase criterion, and 35% were willing to pay more for sustainable products or services.

This shift in consumer preferences has propelled many beauty brands to set environmental goals: to move away from single-use and virgin plastics, provide recyclable, reusable and refillable packaging and offer more transparency around products’ ingredients so customers can ascertain how “green” their purchase is.

However, consumers still struggle to understand the sustainability credentials of many products, according to the British Beauty Council. This is because the industry’s clean-up efforts have been inconsistent, and fall short of making a recognizable impact in the absence of collective goal-setting, global strategy and standardized regulations.

There is no international standard for the beauty industry on how much product ingredient information to share with customers — or how to do so. Brands can set their own rules and goals, giving rise to confusion and “greenwashing,” where sustainability claims are often touted but not substantiated.

Companies often use marketing language like “clean beauty” to make it seem like their products are natural, for example, when they may not actually be organic, sustainable or ethically made.

After rebranding in the West, many beauty companies are still offering to 'whiten' skin elsewhere

“The term ‘clean beauty’ has become quite dangerous. It’s used to sell more products,” according to British Beauty Council CEO Millie Kendall, who added that such buzzwords are losing traction in the UK as British customers wise up to their shortcomings. “Customers need better marketing information and certification information.”

In a 2021 report calling on the industry to have “the courage to change” their business practices, the British Beauty Council wrote that, all too often, even natural ingredients involved in manufacturing products give way to “over-consumption, non-regenerative farming practices, pollution, waste and neglect.”

“The only way out of this is transparency,” Kendall told CNN.

Jen Lee, chief impact officer at US-based brand Beautycounter, said she continues to see confusion over ingredients among consumers. (In 2013, the company launched and published “The Never List,” which currently cites more than 2,800 chemicals — including heavy metals, parabens and formaldehyde — it claims to never use in its products.)

“Natural vs. synthetic ingredients has been a conversation. People think natural is safer, but it’s not always the case,” Lee explained. “Natural ingredients formulated in the industry can have toxic load. Heavy metals can occur in natural components of the earth.”

Mother loses peripheral vision from apparent exposure to mercury in beauty creams. Toxic levels in her home put family at risk, say experts

“We used to be more natural and organic,” added Sasha Plavsic, founder of makeup brand ILIA Beauty. “What was challenging is (that) raw materials were difficult to source or would come in inconsistently or products wouldn’t perform.”

Most makeup is created and molded at high temperatures, Plavsic explained. Purely organic materials often fall apart in this heat, leading to inconsistent results and subpar product performance. “Not every synthetic is bad,” Plavsic said. “Sometimes, it helps create the best in class formula.”

The industry’s plastic packaging is a particular sustainability challenge — 95% is thrown away and the vast majority is not recycled, according to the British Beauty Council.

The cosmetics business is the fourth biggest plastic packaging user globally — after food and beverage, industrial packaging and pharmaceuticals — and plastic is about 67% of the industry’s packaging volume, according to Vantage Market Research. Beauty giant L’Oreal used 144,430 metric tons of plastic in its packaging material in 2021, for example, according to the Ellen Macarthur Foundation (EMF). Estee Lauder Companies reported its brands produced 71,600 metric tons of plastic in product packaging that same year.

And only 9% of the global plastic waste is recycled, according to a report from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development. The United States only recycles 4% of its plastic waste.

Single-use plastic is wreaking havoc on the planet. Here's what you can do to minimize your impact

Many brands are trying to phase out harmful plastics from their operations and adopt post-consumer recycled (PCR) plastic. (L’Oreal has set a target of 50% PCR plastic usage by 2025, while Estee Lauder is targeting 25% “or more” PCR plastic — but both are far from achieving their targets.)

“Between 60-70 major global brands have made unprecedented progress” in PCR plastic usage across industries, EMF’s Plastic Initiative Lead Sander DeFruyt told CNN. But DeFruyt stressed that PCR plastic must be adopted in conjunction with brands removing single and virgin plastics from their usage cycles to truly make a difference.

However, PCR plastic is not easy to find — low recycling rates around the world mean there is limited supply. Meanwhile, demand for it is growing demand across industries, DeFruyt said. This competition hikes up its price, which is already higher than virgin plastic.

Hair care brand FEKKAI claims that it used up to 95% PCR content in its packaging, but pricing and supply issues posed a challenge, forcing it to currently aim for containers and packaging that feature at least 50% PCR in its packaging.

“PCR plastic is more expensive than stock plastic. The cost is hard and then sourcing it is too,” founder Frédéric Fekkai told CNN. “PCR is close to our heart, but there is a massive demand, so finding recycled plastic is difficult.”

Beauty retailers plays a pivotal — and under-utilized — role, with control over stocking decisions and supply chains. But many vary when it comes to the standards they set for brands they sell.

“Smaller businesses do more, full stop,” said Jessi Baker, founder of the technology platform Provenance, which helps brands display their sustainability credentials for customers. “They move more nimbly. Some of them are born-good brands — climate friendliness was part of their setup. They don’t need to restructure their entire supply chain. Their culture already has it compared to the larger brands who need to work hard to change.”

A history of beauty trends -- and the standards that shaped them

Sephora launched its “Clean + Planet Positive” initiative in 2021, which labeled products that met its set criteria. (This is separate from the French retailer’s “Clean at Sephora” program, which is currently facing a consumer lawsuit alleging it carries a significant percentage of products understood by customers to be harmful.) Target launched a similar program in 2022, featuring a “Target Zero” icon for both online and in-store offerings that either have reusable, recyclable, compostable or reduced plastic packaging, or feature waterless or concentrated products.

Still, many steps taken by brands and retailers do not even begin to touch on the waste and pollution generated throughout supply chains, manufacturing and shipping, all huge problems for the industry to grapple with.

The gaps in standardization in the beauty ecosystem can, to some extent, be filled by certifications such as the US-born B Corporation, or B Corp. This accreditation, one of the most well-known in the beauty space, is issued by the non-profit B Lab, which scores a company on a variety of criteria around ethics and sustainability. However beneficial it may be among eco-conscious consumers, though, it is currently completely voluntary for brands to apply for.

Governments and multinationals enforcing regulations and setting a base line for brands to operate from when making sustainability claims would go a long way to making change, many experts and business leaders believe.

Susanne Kaufmann, founder of her namesake beauty brand, says her efforts in Austria would reap better results if more countries around the world had stricter, more uniform garbage disposal laws.

A trash heap 62 meters high shows the scale of India's climate challenge

“I package our product in a recyclable material,” Kaufmann said. (Her products’ packaging, which is refillable and reusable, is made from 75% recycled plastic — and is 100% recyclable.) If I send this to the US, the garbage is not separated… and it’s not recyclable,” she explained, referring to inconsistencies in recycling laws across the United States.

And when it comes to ingredients, the European Chemicals Agency lists 2,495 substances banned from use in cosmetic products marketed for sale or use in the bloc. But the US Food and Drug administration only lists 11, making it more challenging for American consumers to find safer, greener options. The Environmental Working Group, a non-profit watchdog, studied lab tests of 51 sunscreen products in 2021 and found that only 35% of products met the EU standard, compared with 94% that passed the US standard.

However, while government can set minimum requirements, Mia Davis, vice president of sustainability and impact at beauty retailer Credo Beauty, says the needle will move in the private sector.

Sustainable ships: The world's most eco-conscious cruises

“Regulation can raise the floor a bit. A person who doesn’t know about any (sustainability issues) should still be able to walk into a bodega and get clean products… But that’s never going to be what the market can do,” she said. “Market leadership is key.”

In the absence of bold regulations or global standards on sustainability practices, this “leadership” — undertaken both by brands and customers in the beauty marketplace — is likely to be the most immediately impactful vector for addressing the industry’s climate shortcomings. It will take continued collective advocacy and initiative to see meaningful climate-conscious change.

#159. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#158. ‘Forever our Fiona’: fans of the famous hippo mourn her death at zoo

Flowers and notes appeared next to sculpture of hippos at Cincinatti zoo within hours of Fiona dying, days after swallowing a visitors’ hat

Tributes are pouring in and an impromptu shrine has popped up at the Cincinnati zoo in Ohio as fans of the famous hippopotamus Fiona mourn the animal’s death on Thursday, following failed attempts to save her after she ate a knitted hat.

Flowers and notes began appearing next to a sculpture of hippos at the zoo within hours of Fiona dying on Thursday despite numerous treatments aiming to help her eliminate the object that got stuck in her digestive tract.

One note with flowers said: “Forever our Fiona”. Amy Hackey said to Cincinnati’s Channel 7 TV station: “I thought she was going to make it.” As the tributes continued arriving, Hackey added: “I think it will be our equivalent of Princess Di”, referring to the late Diana, Princess of Wales, the first wife of Britain’s King Charles, whose sudden death in a car crash in 1997 prompted an outpouring of grief and tributes.

Yolanda Gould, who was visiting from Kentucky, told the TV station that she’d been following Fiona’s story since she was born, when her survival after a premature birth made her famous and a symbol of strength for many.

“Cincinnati has such an incredible bond with her because of her journey and what’s she’s been through. She taught us all to be resilient.”

The nine-year-old hippopotamus earlier in the week mistakenly swallowed a visitor’s hat that had accidentally fallen into her enclosure. It was not possible to operate on the adult animal.

Her zoo keepers placed her under surveillance and she was initially asymptomatic, then her appetite waned. Despite round-the-clock care from the zoo’s veterinary team and care staff, and various treatments, the Nile hippo became increasingly sick and later on Thursday it was announced that she was dead.

Fiona became a global sensation in 2017 as a tiny newborn who was unable to stand on her own and weighed just 29lbs. As her legend grew, she inspired books and a line of beer.

The Cincinnati zoo was also where 10 years ago bouquets and tributes accumulated after zoo keepers were obliged to shoot dead an adult gorilla, named Harambe. The male western lowland gorilla, 17, was executed in 2016 after a child fell into his enclosure and was grasped by Harambe.

#156. Germany face belgium in Nations League quarterfinals — Klopp plays down favorites tag

Germany coach Jürgen Klopp is looking to "carry positive momentum forward" as his team draw neighboring Belgium in the quarterfinals of the UEFA Nations League.

Germany, who finished top of Group A2 following their 5-0 win over the Netherlands in Berlin on Monday are to face Belgium in the Nations League quarterfinals—after they finished second in Group A1. The Belgians recently came up with a statement result of their own, beating Italy 4-1. The match-up between the neighboring countries promises to be closely contested, with Belgium sitting at No. 8 in the men's rankings, three places above Germany.

The head coach of Belgium is former Bayern Munich player Mark van Bommel, who took over from Rudi Garcia following the World Cup, where they were knocked out by eventual champions Spain in the quarterfinals.

Germany head into the knockout phase on a high note, not only because they won their group, but also the way they performed in their last two matches, against Serbia and the Netherlands in the group stage.

Germany head coach Jürgen Klopp followed the draw, held in Nyon on Friday, from the German FA's (DFB) Frankfurt offices. He played down the notion that Germany enter the tie as the favorites.

"I've practically never given it any thought. It's never interested me," he said.

"An awful lot can happen in that time. We're very happy with the final impression we left, yet we're also fully aware that we're only at the very beginning of a development process. We want to carry this positive momentum forward—there's still a great deal to be done."

The matches are slated for March 25 and March 30, 2027, and having won their group, Germany will have home-field advantage for the second leg.

Germany and Belgium have faced each other 26 times to date, with West Germany and Germany having the upper hand with 20 wins, five defeats and one draw.

Should Germany advance to the semifinals, they will go on to face the winner of England and Norway – a World Cup quarterfinals rematch.

In the other two semifinals matchups, the Netherlands drew Portugal, while the top pairing sees Spain face France in a rematch of one of last summer's World Cup semifinals.

The "Final Four" mini tournament is to be held from June 9 to 13 but the host venue won't be determined until the semifinal lineup has been completed.

#153. US Senate approves bill to speed up permitting reviews for energy projects and datacenters

Bipartisan legislation now goes to the House amid concerns by some Democrats and environmental groups

The Senate approved bipartisan legislation on Thursday aimed at speeding up permitting reviews to build power plants, transmission lines and other energy infrastructure as lawmakers seek to increase the nation’s energy supplies to meet surging demand and lower electricity prices.

The legislation’s sweeping changes would shorten the timeline to start big energy projects and make sure data centers pay for the energy they use. Anger over datacenters and rising electricity prices helped usher in a wave of Democrats pledging to clamp down on the energy-hungry behemoths.

The 62-33 vote is the first major action by Congress since midterm elections that soundly repudiated Donald Trump and congressional Republicans. The bill now goes to the House, which approved a more limited, industry-friendly version last year.

The Senate bill would overhaul several environmental laws to make it easier to build energy infrastructure, drawing criticism from Democrats and environmental groups about limits on judicial review and changes to bedrock laws such as the National Environmental Policy Act, Clean Water Act and Endangered Species Act.

Trump urged approval of the bill, calling it a “BIG WIN for US ENERGY INDEPENDENCE and DOMINANCE” in a recent social media post. The bill, formally known as the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026, will ensure the US can build pipelines, mines and power plants more quickly, “lowering Energy Costs for American Families”, Trump wrote.

Fifteen Democrats, including Senate minority leader Chuck Schumer, voted for the bill. Two Republicans, Josh Hawley of Missouri and Mike Rounds of South Dakota, voted no.

Major industry groups representing developers in clean energy and the oil and gas sector support the bill as a way to deliver more electricity to the grid and avoid delays for infrastructure projects that can stretch a decade or more.

However, many environmental organizations have denounced the measure, saying it would lock in fossil fuel development, weaken environmental safeguards and make it harder for communities to challenge energy project approvals.

“Make no mistake: This backroom deal will pave the way for even more fossil fuel projects like gas power plants for data centers, oil and gas pipelines and (liquefied natural gas) terminals while sacrificing fundamental and common-sense public health and environmental protections for communities across the US,” said Jen Duggan, executive director of the Environmental Integrity Project.

Benefits for renewable energy are outweighed by likely increases in greenhouse gas emissions and air and water pollution from the fossil fuel industry, Duggan and other critics said. She called the bill “a gift to some of the biggest polluters in America” and complained that it was being rushed through a lame-duck Congress without public hearings or sufficient debate.

A final vote on the measure was held up this week as Oregon’s two Democratic senators pushed to add amendments, including one to ban datacenters on public lands.

Senators Jeff Merkley and Ron Wyden are among several Democrats in both chambers who say the party should use its stronger position in the next Congress to pass legislation that boosts clean energy, such as solar and wind power, while limiting expansion of oil, natural gas and other fossil fuels.

“Getting more renewable electrons where they are needed doesn’t tackle climate change if you simultaneously promote the burning of more fossil fuels. Unfortunately, this bill does exactly that,” Merkley said.

Majority Republicans stymied Merkley’s effort to ensure votes on a host of amendments, including one that would block the Trump administration from continuing to cancel clean energy grants and other Democratic priorities for “purely political purposes”.

The bill’s sponsors reneged on promises of “a robust amendment process”, he said. In the end, the measure was approved without amendments offered by rank-and-file senators.

“I do think that this bill is very consequential to the future of this planet,” Merkley said.

Major environmental groups, including the Sierra Club and National Wildlife Federation, oppose the bill, citing concerns about limits on judicial review and changes to the National Environmental Policy Act and other laws. The environmental policy law, known as Nepa, requires agencies to consider a project’s possible impacts and allows the public to be heard before a project is approved. The current review process often takes five years or more, and shortening that time frame is a key goal of the bill.

“In America today, it takes too long and costs too much to build,” said Wyoming senator John Barrasso, the No 2 Republican in the Senate. “Energy projects that would help lower prices remain frozen for years” as they await approval by the federal government.

“Construction cannot start without first spending years in the regulatory penalty box,” driving up costs and creating uncertainty, Barrasso said.

The bill’s four main champions – two Democrats and two Republicans – have released a detailed set of amendments designed to bolster support for the legislation.

The four senators, who lead the chamber’s energy and environment panels, negotiated the deal a few weeks before the election. Republican senators Shelley Moore Capito of West Virginia and Mike Lee of Utah and Democratic senators Martin Heinrich of New Mexico and Sheldon Whitehouse of Rhode Island say Americans are tired of paying more in utility bills while projects that could lower costs sit stuck in the planning phase for years.

“We are going to make it easier to build in America again, and make life more affordable for American families,” Lee said.

Whitehouse, a longtime climate hawk who has been criticized by some allies for supporting the permitting bill, said the measure is “the floor, not the ceiling, of our climate and environmental aspirations”, adding that he hopes “to accomplish big things with our big Democratic majorities” over the next two years.

But Democratic senator Maria Cantwell of Washington state said the bill has been rushed and its impacts are not fully considered.

Cantwell, who met Tuesday with tribal, outdoor recreation and conservation leaders, said the bill would undermine tribal nations and weaken protections for the nation’s bodies of water, land and wildlife.

“Now is the time to slow this process down, to actually have a formal process” to debate and amend the bill, she said. “Don’t sacrifice the Clean Water Act or hunting or fishing grounds, historic or sacred sites, or protections of tribal sovereignty. We all need new sources of energy and power. But this bill, as it’s currently drafted, is not the answer.”

#152. Is Pakistan backing anti-Taliban groups in Afghanistan?

The Taliban rulers in Afghanistan have accused Pakistan of aiding and sheltering armed resistance groups. The allegations come amid heightened tensions and are likely to deepen the rift between the two neighbors.

The Taliban authorities in Afghanistan claim that Islamabad is supporting armed Afghan groups that are opposed to their rule, an accusation the Pakistani government denies.

Taliban Foreign Minister Amir Khan Muttaqi recently alleged that Pakistan had launched a "project to destabilize Afghanistan."

He claimed that "thousands of armed individuals have gathered along the Durand Line (Afghanistan-Pakistan border) to undermine the stability achieved in Afghanistan."

The accusations come after months of border clashes between Afghan Taliban and Pakistani forces.

In July, the Afghanistan Freedom Front (AFF), an anti-Taliban group, reportedly clashed with Taliban forces in the Zebak district of Badakhshan, a northeastern Afghan province. The group said it also carried out attacks in Nooristan and Kunar provinces in October.

Another "resistance group," the United Front of Afghanistan, claimed it attacked Taliban forces in Kandahar province along the border with Pakistan.

"The people who created insecurity in Badakhshan crossed from Chitral [in Pakistan] and later fled back to Chitral. They had been brought in. Even Pakistani military personnel were with them," alleged Zabihullah Mujahid, Taliban's main spokesperson, in an interview with DW's Pashto-Dari service in September.

Pakistan's Ministry of Information swiftly dismissed the allegations as propaganda.

Groups that oppose the Taliban rule in Afghanistan say they are not supported by any external force, and their resistance is purely indigenous.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

More than five years since returning to power in Kabul, the Taliban have consolidated their control over Afghanistan and largely suppressed both armed resistance groups and political dissidents.

Shinkai Karokhail, a former Afghan member of parliament, told DW it would be logistically difficult for anti-Taliban Afghan groups to cross the Pakistani border and enter Afghanistan without Islamabad's support, referring to clashes in Badakhshan and Nooristan provinces.

The Taliban themselves maintained close ties with Pakistan in the 1990s during their first stint in power. Some US officials even claimed that Islamabad didn't relinquish its support to the Islamist group even after September 11, 2001, terrorist attacks in the United States. Islamabad has always refuted these allegations.

But Afrasiab Khattak, a Pakistani political analyst and former senator, believes that Pakistani support for anti-Taliban groups is "real."

"Taliban opponents come to Islamabad, hold meetings, and it is a part of Pakistan's policy toward Afghanistan," he told DW.

Qandil Abbas, an associate professor of politics and international relations at Quaid-i-Azam University in Islamabad, disagrees.

"Pakistan may provide political support [to Taliban opponents], but not military support," she told DW.

Ali Ahmad Jalali, a professor at the Near East South Asia Center for Strategic Studies at the National Defense University in Washington DC, said it is possible that Islamabad is backing Taliban opponents as a "leverage."

"When such incidents take place along the Afghanistan-Pakistan border, and given Pakistan's history of support for Afghan groups, it would not be surprising if Pakistan is supporting these groups," Jalali said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Pakistani officials have repeatedly demanded that the Taliban take action against anti-Pakistan militant groups, particularly the Tehreek-e-Taliban Pakistan (TTP), a militant group responsible for attacking Pakistani civilians and soldiers, and the Baloch Liberation Army (BLA), an outlawed armed separatist group active in its western Balochistan province.

Islamabad alleges that these groups organize and launch attacks inside Pakistan from Afghan territory with Taliban support.

"Meaningful progress requires Afghan authorities to address legitimate security concerns of neighboring countries, particularly the threat of terrorism," Pakistani Foreign Minister Ishaq Dar said this week at the Economic Cooperation Organization (ECO) summit in Islamabad.

Islamabad said on Friday that Afghanistan was not invited to participate in the ECO gathering as Pakistan does not recognize the Taliban regime in Kabul.

Analyst Jalali, who served as Afghanistan's interior minister from 2003 to 2005, argues that Islamabad is demanding something the Taliban may neither be willing nor able to provide.

"Pakistan wants the Taliban to cooperate in fighting against groups such as the TTP. But the TTP has long fought alongside the Afghan Taliban. There are ideological as well as friendly ties between the two. They seek a similar political system, and some Afghan Taliban members may even be among TTP fighters," he added.

Analysts also point to the Taliban's efforts to strengthen relations with India, Pakistan's long-standing regional rival, as a cause of concern for Islamabad.

Malek Sitez, Afghan expert and researcher of international relations, argues that Pakistan, among other things, doesn't want Kabul to be close to New Delhi. But Pakistan no longer has the kind of influence over Afghanistan that it had in the past, he said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Despite these accusations and counteraccusations from both sides, Jalali believes Pakistan still does not seek to remove the Taliban from power.

"I do not think Pakistan can or wants to overthrow the Taliban and replace them with another group," he underlined. Instead, he stressed, Islamabad's objective is to pressure the Taliban into cooperating against the TTP.

Aziz Barez, a political analyst of Afghanistan and regional affairs, is of the view that Afghan groups opposed to the Taliban can also not fully trust Islamabad, adding that they may welcome alleged Pakistani support but are also aware that such backing could be temporary.

"It is possible that Pakistan and the Taliban will resolve their differences tomorrow and become close allies again," he told DW.

Ahmad Waheed Ahmady and Helay Asad contributed to this report.

#151. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#150. 'It's a dream come true' – South African DJ celebrates featuring in GTA 6

DJ Lag is one of South Africa's best-known electronic music producers

South African DJ Lag says it is a "dream come true" to have his music featured on Grand Theft Auto 6 (GTA 6), the hugely popular video game released on Thursday.

DJ lag, one of South Africa's best-known electronic music producers, is among several African stars to feature, alongside Nigerian singers Burna Boy and Asake.

"I can't explain the feeling. I started playing the game last night and it's amazing," DJ Lag told the BBC's Newsday programme, adding that he had been playing GTA since he was about 10 years old and could hardly believe he was now part of the latest edition.

Many players have already had a chance to play the game following its worldwide launch.

The game, which lets players explore a fictional American city, features a dedicated African music radio station playing Afrobeats, amapiano and other sounds from across the continent.

The release of GTA 6 ends a 13-year wait for a new edition of the game.

GTA 6 is a satirical video series associated with crime and violence

Rockstar Games, the developer behind the franchise, has revealed details of the game's soundtrack and some in-game radio stations, but has yet to release a complete list of songs featured in the game.

Rockstar says AfroBank FM features everything from 20th Century African classics to contemporary amapiano and 3-step, giving the continent's music a prominent place in the latest edition.

Burna Boy co-hosts the station alongside British DJ and producer Palms Trax, while Asake's hit song Joha is among the tracks players can listen to as they drive around the game's virtual world.

DJ Lag, whose real name is Lwazi Asanda Gwala, is regarded as one of the pioneers of gqom, a genre of electronic dance music that emerged in the port city of Durban in the early 2010s.

Ghanaian highlife legends Pat Thomas and Ebo Taylor also feature, alongside South African artists Robot Boii and Kamo Mphela, bringing a mix of classic and contemporary African music to one of gaming's biggest franchises.

Rockstar has also announced a 34-track official soundtrack album, released in partnership with Atlantic Records, featuring an original Burna Boy song titled Bigger.

For African fans, the inclusion of familiar music adds another dimension to the experience, allowing players to hear some of the continent's biggest sounds while exploring the American setting.

The series allows players to roam through sprawling fictional cities, drive vehicles and complete missions involving crime, police pursuits and other adventures.

The latest instalment takes players to Vice City, a fictional version of Miami, and the surrounding US state of Leonida, inspired by Florida.

Its story follows two criminals, Jason and Lucia, who become caught up in a wider conspiracy after a robbery goes wrong.

The launch has generated enormous excitement among gamers around the world.

Some fans downloaded it in advance so they could begin playing as soon as it became available, while others have booked leave from work.

The previous edition, GTA 5, was one of the best-selling video games of all time, selling more than 200 million copies worldwide.

The new release is expected to be another major commercial success, although it remains to be seen whether it will match its predecessor's extraordinary sales.

#148. Mahmood ‘smells a rat’ over housing provision for vulnerable people in Birmingham

Warnings over exploitation risks come amid push for investigation into supported exempt accommodation sector

The home secretary has launched an attack on providers of housing for vulnerable residents in her Birmingham constituency, saying she “smells a rat” after warnings over spiralling costs and risks of exploitation.

A push for an independent investigation into the supported exempt accommodation (SEA) sector in Birmingham, one of the largest in the country, is under way.

The SEA system is designed to support vulnerable people with additional needs, such as people with addiction, women fleeing domestic abuse and former prisoners, by providing them with housing as well as care, support and supervision.

However, politicians, charities and residents have raised concerns about the sector, including the existence of rogue providers who fail to provide the required support, as well as the risks to vulnerable residents of exploitation and antisocial behaviour. This week, an internal examination at Birmingham city council found more than £615m in SEA housing benefit had been paid in 15 months without “timely evidence” that relevant support had been given to residents.

Shabana Mahmood, the MP for Birmingham Ladywood, told the Birmingham Mail: “I see the impact of supported exempt accommodation all over the patch, and the use of it and the abuse I know happens in this sector is causing many of the issues in my communities.

“When you look at what is going on in the rest of the country we are such an outlier … I smell a rat.”

Mahmood said she was talking to Pat Mc Fadden, the work and pensions secretary,about the enhanced housing benefits that fund the sector.

Birmingham has more than 34,000 people living in SEA across approximately 12,200 properties, far outstripping the 22,000 SEA residents in London, despite the capital having a population seven times larger than Birmingham.

Some have argued increased demand and need in the city has made it a hotspot for SEA. However, others have criticised the local council for a lack of oversight and compliance, as well as national government for poor regulation.

Mahmood said she had regularly shared her concerns with the city council, including to leaders during the council’s 14-year Labour administration. “I can’t tell you how much I fought with my former Labour colleagues over this issue,” she said.

An independent review into the SEA sector, commissioned by the council’s cross-party audit committee and published this week, found in the year commencing April 2024 the council had paid £404.4m in SEA housing benefit. In a four-month period from April this year, the council had paid a further £211.8m – totalling more than £615m across 15 months.

The review assessed the local authority’s approach to the SEA sector and concluded it was not fully effective. “Risks are not always effectively managed, which may impact the achievement of objectives in some areas,” it said.

In particular, the report found 12,700 live cases were awaiting a review to prove care, support and supervision were being given to residents in SEA after the tenancy had commenced. This meant the council continued to provide enhanced housing benefit payments “without timely evidence” that relevant support was being given to residents.

Our morning email breaks down the key stories of the day, telling you what’s happening and why it matters

The review also found, in some cases, SEA housing benefit claims were active for more than a decade, some more than 20 years, without being subject to eligibility checks.

Marcus Bernasconi, a Labour councillor, backed the need for an independent investigation and said the review showed the council had “totally lost control of supported accommodation with the bill for the taxpayer running into the hundreds of millions of pounds with no idea if genuine support is being provided”.

Government legislation to strengthen regulation in the sector, including a new licensing scheme, was passed in 2023 but remains under consultation.

A Birmingham city council spokesperson said the independent review “provides assurance that the council’s process for assessing housing benefit claims is robust and includes additional controls beyond what other councils have. The report also sets out measures the council will implement to improve monitoring and consistency across its processes relating to supported exempt accommodation.

“However, it remains the case that there is no adequate regulation for the council to effectively regulate the supported exempt accommodation sector in Birmingham. Even if the council had the additional measures mentioned in the audit report in place before, there would be no guarantee they would have impacted the supported exempt sector in Birmingham.”

A government spokesperson said: “We’ve been monitoring issues around supported housing in Birmingham closely and we’re actively considering next steps to ensure that all supported housing in the city is high quality, and that poor providers cannot take advantage of the system designed to help the most vulnerable.

“More broadly, we are taking action to raise standards in the sector – including ensuring councils have stronger powers to fine non-compliant landlords.”

#147. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#145. Measles in America: Here's a constructive approach to beating the highly contagious disease, based on Brazil's experience

The US is now officially a measles contagion zone. By mid-2026, there were more infections than in all of the previous year. But it could beat measles again, just like Brazil did two years ago.

Health experts saw it coming — the fact that after 26 years, the US would lose its status as measles-free. Back in July 2026, DW reported how one epidemiologist had suggested an 'amnesia' about the measles risk was pushing a growth in US infections.

Four months later, it was official. On November 17, 2026, the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) said the US had lost its measles elimination status along with Mexico.

But it doesn't have to stay like that for long.

Look at Brazil, where in 2019, with more than 21,000 measles cases, the country also lost its measles-free status. But within five years — during the COVID-19 pandemic — it fought and won back its measles-free status. And since 2024, Brazil has stayed measles-free, even as the highly contagious disease spread across the rest of the Americas.

In 2026, with the data available up to the time of writing this article, Brazil had had only 70 measles cases. They were all linked to infections brought in from abroad, often from neighboring Peru.

Brazil has fewer people than the US, about 213 million compared with the US's nearly 350 million, but the two countries are similar in size. So, the comparison is worth considering.

Brazil moves fast. The PAHO asks countries to respond within 72 hours of a suspected case. Brazil's own rule is a ladder:

It has also made hunting for cases a national habit.

In a program called "Dia S", health workers knock on doors and visit schools, daycare centers, and churches to find people who may be sick but have not seen a doctor.

Brazil holds targeted, mass vaccination days called "Dia D". There was one in November, in the area where Brazil borders Colombia and Peru.

If someone is diagnosed with measles, health workers try to track down every person the patient may have met ("contact persons") and they vaccinate them and other people living nearby.

The country has so far provided more than 16 million doses of measles vaccination in 2026 and has donated 500,000 doses to Peru.

Brazil isn't perfect, though. According to official estimates from the World Health Organization, only 76% of children got both required doses of the vaccine in 2025 — well short of the 95% target. Experts warn this leaves the country at risk because measles is so highly contagious; a country needs to be as close to 100% immunized as possible to protect people and stop the disease from spreading.

"Elimination is not a trophy that we win once and put on a shelf. It must be protected every day," says PAHO Director Jarbas Barbosa.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The US lost its measles-free status because the PAHO found that the same strain of the virus had been spreading in the country for 12 months or more. Mexico and Bolivia lost their status on the same day as the US.

The US has had more than 5,400 cases in 2026 (at time of writing), more than twice as many as in all of 2025, and about 19 times as many as in 2024. Nearly everyone who got sick with measles was unvaccinated or had an unknown vaccination status.

In August 2026, when US President Donald Trump signed an executive order on new vaccination recommendations for children, it came at a time, when, as we reported, experts said people had stopped getting vaccinated against measles because infection rates were low. As PAHO's Jarbas Barbosa says above, avoiding vaccination when infection rates are low is a mistake.

To be fair, Trump's executive order listed the measles vaccine in a top category of vaccinations recommended for every child. But the administration's stance on vaccination in general is unclear; Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. has often declared opposition to vaccination, including against measles, advising people to take vitamin A instead. In Texas, this led to children being hospitalized with toxic levels of the supplement, and left them with no protection against measles.

Before the COVID-19 pandemic, 95% of US kindergartners had their measles shots. Now, it's 92%, according to the US Centers for Disease Control and Protection (CDC). That leaves about 280,000 kindergartners unprotected.

In the Texas counties, where a 2025 measles outbreak began, coverage was as low as 77% at the time.

US children usually need vaccines to start school, but most states let parents opt out for religious or personal reasons, with no doctor involved. In 24 states, so many children were exempt that those states couldn't reach the 95% threshold even if every other child got vaccinated.

But vaccination rates have fallen elsewhere in the world as well, including in Europe. In Germany, where measles infections have also risen, experts blame anti-vaccination rhetoric that emerged during the COVID-19 pandemic.

A person sick with measles can infect up to18 other, unprotected people. With vaccination, it is possible to increase the number of protected people and lower the number of people the sick person can infect.

To prevent a measles outbreak, a community needs about 95% of people to be vaccinated and/or immune. The vaccination consists of two doses of the MMR (measles, mumps, rubella) vaccine.

But even after the two doses, a person can expect to have only 97% protection against measles — 3% short of full protection — so, nearly everyone in the community has to be vaccinated to keep the average level of protect as close to 100% as possible.

"For some diseases like measles, even a small decrease in vaccination rates can lead to more outbreaks of disease [so] community immunity requires over 95% of susceptible individuals to be immunized," says Andrew Racine, president of the American Academy of Pediatrics.

And the 95% has to hold everywhere. A good national average can be affected by, for example, a single school where only 70% of the children are vaccinated. And that is where the outbreak start is likely to start.

In the US, where vaccination rates at kindergartens have dropped to 92%, that small difference is big enough to cause a measles outbreak.

Around the world, measles deaths have dropped by 88% since 2000, thanks to vaccines.

But progress has stalled. Only about three out of four children worldwide get both doses of the vaccine. The biggest outbreaks right now are in Bangladesh, Indonesia and Guatemala.

Data from Europe shows that things can get better. In the European Union, cases fell from more than 35,000 in 2024 to about 2,300 the following year, with no measles-related deaths.

Romania went from more than 30,000 cases in 2024 to about 4,200 in 2025.

Hungary, where 99% of children are vaccinated, there have been no measles cases in the past year.

But in January, six European countries lost their measles-free status, including the UK, Spain and Austria. Across Europe, eight out of ten people who got measles in 2025 were unvaccinated.

The Americas are seeing a major surge in measles infections, with more than 58,000 cases in 2026, over four times as many as this time (November) in 2025. Guatemala has been hit hardest, with almost 35,000 cases.

The deadliest outbreaks are in Africa. African Union countries have so far reported nearly 1,800 measles deaths in 2026, about 1,650 of them in Democratic Republic of Congo. Yet, Sudan, despite the war, raised first-dose measles coverage by 22 percentage points in 2025, which was the world's largest gain.

Measles is extremely contagious. One person can infect up to 18 unprotected people, which makes it about 12 times more contagious than the flu.

The virus can linger in the air for up to two hours after a sick person has left the room. And people spread it before they know they are sick. Someone with measles is contagious for four days before the rash appears and four days after.

It's not just a rash. About one in five unvaccinated people who get measles in the US ends up in the hospital. Measles can weaken the immune system for years, making it harder to fight off other infections.

But it's preventable. Two doses of the MMR vaccine (which also protects against mumps and rubella) protect 97% of people for life.
