#116. Israeli military completes ground operation in Gaza beyond Yellow Line

Residents fled the area near Nuseirat and Bureij refugee camps following the Israeli evacuation order

Israeli forces have completed a rare ground operation in part of central Gaza outside their control, the military says, a day after residents were ordered to evacuate.

Families fled their homes and tents on Wednesday as tanks advanced into an area east of Nuseirat refugee camp and onto the territory's main north-south road under the cover of artillery strikes.

The Israeli military said the "targeted" operation aimed to dismantle a "terror tunnel" west of the so-called Yellow Line, which marks the more than 60% of Gaza's territory occupied by Israel.

The Palestinian armed group Hamas, which controls the rest of Gaza, accused Israel of a "flagrant violation" of the October 2025 ceasefire deal.

Violence has continued in Gaza over the past 13 months, with near-daily Israeli strikes, and living conditions for the 2.1 million people there remain dire.

Wednesday's Israeli incursion deep beyond the Yellow Line was reportedly the first to be publicly announced by the Israel Defense Forces (IDF) since the ceasefire began.

In the early afternoon, Israeli military body Cogat's Arabic spokesperson announced the start of the operation and warned Palestinians living in an area north of Nuseirat and Bureij refugee camp to stay away for their own safety. It also said a section of Salah al-Din road that passed through the area would be closed to traffic.

In the evening, the IDF said the joint operation with the Shin Bet security service sought "to dismantle a terror tunnel west of the Yellow Line that posed an immediate and direct threat to IDF troops".

On Thursday morning, Cogat told Palestinians on social media that "the activity has ended, and the Salah al-Din axis has been reopened".

Hamas warned in a statement that the advance of Israeli military vehicles beyond the Yellow Line and the evacuation of residents constituted "a dangerous development and a flagrant violation of the ceasefire agreement".

Gaza's Hamas-run government media office meanwhile said that an estimated 6,000 people had fled their homes and tents because of the operation, and that this amounted to the war crime of forced displacement.

Footage published by Palestinian media outlets and on social media showed dozens of families fleeing areas near Nuseirat camp following the Israeli evacuation order.

In one video verified by BBC Verify, a man could be seen ducking after hearing artillery fire and then running along Salah al-Din road, about 1.5km (0.9 miles) west of the Yellow Line. "The Israeli occupation now is firing randomly near the houses and tents of people," he said.

An image, meanwhile, showed six armoured vehicles, including tanks and bulldozers, around Salah al-Din road.

A resident of Nuseirat who was forced to flee told BBC Arabic's Middle East Daily programme: "The situation is now extremely dire, with Israeli forces firing indiscriminately at areas where displaced people have sought shelter."

"We couldn't take any of our belongings with us - not even our blankets, pillows, or clothes. We had no choice but to leave everything behind."

A second resident also reported "indiscriminate gunfire and shelling" and said there had been "numerous casualties, including fatalities".

Asked about those reports, the IDF sent a general statement about the operation.

Multiple verified videos of the aftermath of the incursion on Thursday showed destruction, earth banks, and heavy vehicle tracks more than 1.2km beyond the Yellow Line.

That is believed to be the furthest Israeli troops have advanced into central Gaza since they occupied the so-called Netzarim Corridor before the ceasefire.

Some residents returned to check their properties following the Israeli withdrawal.

"The entire area has been destroyed, and we honestly do not know what to do," Abd Aliwa told Reuters news agency as he sat beside a pile of rubble.

Israel building new land barriers in Gaza and displacing families

Israel moves Yellow Line deeper into Gaza, satellite images show

Gaza's Hamas-run Civil Defence agency said three people were killed and others were wounded in three separate Israeli drone strikes on Wednesday.

One man was killed in Nuseirat, the second was killed to the north in Gaza City, and the third was killed in the central town of Deir al-Balah, it added.

The IDF told the BBC that it had "struck several military operatives".

Gaza health officials said another two people were killed on Thursday by Israeli fire in eastern Gaza City, according to Reuters. There was no immediate comment from the IDF.

At least 1,662 people have been killed in Israeli attacks in Gaza since the ceasefire began last year, according to the territory's Hamas-run health ministry, whose figures are considered reliable by the UN.

The Israeli military says six of its soldiers have been killed over the same period.

Meanwhile, more than 1.9 million people - about 90% of Gaza's population - remain displaced, with 1.4 million of them living in tents or makeshift shelters, according to the UN.

Reconstruction has yet to begin in Gaza, where an estimated 82% of all structures have been destroyed or damaged during the war, as US President Donald Trump's peace plan has largely stalled.

The disarmament of Hamas has been a key sticking point.

The group has agreed to give up its weapons in exchange for a phased withdrawal of Israeli troops from the 60% of Gaza they currently occupy. But Israel has insisted that Hamas must fully disarm before any pullout.

The war was triggered by the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, when about 1,200 people were killed and 251 others were taken hostage.

Since then, more than 74,710 people have been killed in Israeli attacks in Gaza, its health ministry says.

#115. Myanmar committed genocide against Rohingya minority, world’s top court rules

Judgment finds ‘intent to destroy a substantial part of the Rohingya population’ after case seen as critical test for dealing with most serious of state crimes

Myanmar committed genocide against its Rohingya minority during a 2017 military campaign, the world’s highest court has ruled, in a historic and unprecedented decision.

The judgment from the international court of justice (ICJ) – which has never previously found a state responsible for genocide – ends a seven-year legal battle that was seen as a critical global test for dealing with the most serious of all state crimes.

The ICJ president, Yuji Iwasawa, said a majority of nine judges on the 15-seat court found that “the Republic of the Union of Myanmar has committed genocide against the Rohingya”.

The 2016-17 campaign by Myanmar’s military forced more than 700,000 mostly Muslim Rohingya to flee across the border into Bangladesh, arriving with reports of mass killings of adults and children, gang-rapes and burned-down villages.

Nearly a decade later, in a refugee camp in Bangladesh, crowds of Rohingya people watching on outdoor TV screens leaped up in celebration and applause as the final verdict was read out.

One Rohingya survivor, Yousuf Ali, had travelled to the court to hear the verdict. “Today, we have finally been heard, and it is a profound relief,” he said. “This judgment gives hope to our children and the next generation of Rohingya. But our fight is not over.”

Governments had been anxiously awaiting the decision handed down in the Netherlands, where the UN-run court is based, in part because the ruling will set a precedent for genocide claims, notably a high-profile case against Israel.

In what could become a crucial factor for the Gaza case, where Israel claims its mass killings are part of a counter-terrorism operation, judges dismissed Myanmar’s claims of carrying out legitimate military operations against insurgents.

Genocide, defined after the Nazi Holocaust by a 1948 UN convention, is harder to prove than war crimes because it requires evidence of specific “intent” to destroy a national, ethnic, racial or religious group. Iwasawa said during the reading of the decision that the court was “fully convinced that Myanmar committed acts falling under … the genocide convention with an intent to destroy a substantial part of the Rohingya population in Myanmar”.

After the ruling, Britain’s foreign minister, Ed Miliband, said the government was expelling Myanmar’s defence attaché and called “urgently” for a session at the UN security council, the body that has the power to enforce ICJ rulings.

The case was raised by the Gambia, a Muslim-majority state, on behalf of Myanmar survivors. It rested on claims of genocidal statements and “patterns of conduct” that showed attempts to destroy the group during assaults on Rakhine state, including gunning down people as they fled and blocking their return.

In January, the ICJ held closed-door sessions to hear from survivors who had travelled from camps in Bangladesh. More than a million Rohingya still live there, in the world’s largest refugee settlement, and fewer than 600,000 are estimated to remain in Buddhist-majority Myanmar.

Refugees who had fled to Bangladesh told the Guardian in 2017 of a massacre in the village of Tula Toli, where survivors described systematic shooting of civilians, mass graves and the drowning of children in a river. That village, which satellite footage later showed had been burned down, was one of several analysed by rights investigators and built a case for genocide.

During hearings, the Gambian justice minister, Dawda Jallow, told the court the case was not “about esoteric issues of international law”. “It is about real people, real stories and a real group of human beings,” said Jallow. “The Rohingya of Myanmar. They have been targeted for destruction.”

Legal experts see Thursday’s judgment as a key test for sexual violence in genocide assessments, as the convention says the crime can include acts with the intent to “prevent births”. Investigators have said gang-rapes and soldiers’ mutilation of women’s genitals were carried out intentionally and systematically to stop reproduction.

The court appeared to back that assessment, accusing the military of using sexual violence to “physically and mentally impair the willingness and ability of the [Rohingya] population to procreate”, referring to cultural stigma for rape survivors. Myanmar had also imposed other measures to restrict births, the court ruled, such as limits of two children per Rohingya family.

Myanmar has denied carrying out a genocide, claiming its actions targeted a Rohingya militant group that conducted guerrilla-style attacks. Its military responded with massive “clearance operations”.

However, judges rejected Myanmar’s claim that its actions were primarily intended as counter-terrorism operations, or even that they were an attempt to punish or forcibly displace the population but without committing genocide.

The country’s leader, Min Aung Hlaing, who headed the military during the 2016-17 attacks, has a pending arrest warrant from the international criminal court (ICC), which, unlike the ICJ, prosecutes individuals and not states, on allegations of crimes against humanity.

The former general overthrew the elected government of Aung San Suu Kyi in a 2021 coup, triggering a civil war that has left tens of thousands dead. He was installed as civilian president in April after elections that were widely discredited.

While the ICJ does not have the power to enforce rulings, its decisions are considered binding, carry diplomatic weight and can shape international action, including sanctions.

During a reading of the decision that lasted more than two hours in a packed Great Hall of Justice in The Hague, Iwasawa called for Myanmar to provide reparations and restitution. This included allowing the safe return of Rohingya to their homes, returning or replacing their property, and providing physical and mental rehabilitation.

The ruling was also significant as it formally classified the Rohingya as a distinct ethnic group under international law, something they have been denied by Myanmar, which refers to them as Bengalis or foreign migrants.

Reached by phone shortly after he left the courtroom, the ​lead counsel for the case against Myanmar told the Guardian the mood had been emotional in the room as the judgment was read out in front of his legal team, which included Rohingya.

“This is the ICJ recognising their history of discrimination, recognising them as a group, recognising the horrific violence they endured and suffered,” said Arsalan Suleman. “It was a really emotional moment for them and the whole team. We felt the weight of the court’s judgment.”

Israel and its chief ally, the US, have been closely following the Myanmar case, as the decision provides the strongest hint of how judges could assess the Gaza case, brought by South Africa. UN independent investigations, as well as genocide scholars, have already said Israel has committed acts of genocide in Gaza.

A judgment finding Myanmar had breached the convention has been predicted to lead to the US imposing sanctions on ICJ judges, including this year’s Nobel peace prize winner, Navi Pillay.

Washington had considered the Rohingya crisis to be a genocide, based on a decision by the Biden administration. However, the administration of Donald Trump has been on the offensive against international rights bodies, including imposing sanctions on the ICC.

The US state department said in a statement that it would study the ICJ’s decision “to fully assess its implications” and that “the proper interpretation of the genocide convention is of paramount importance, as is preventing politicisation of the ICJ”.

It reiterated US support for the people of Myanmar and said it shared “grave concerns over the continued abuses of human rights against Rohingya Muslims and members of other minority groups”.

The Trump administration of has been implacably opposed to international legal organisations. On Thursday, the president used a trilateral meeting with Russia and China on Thursday to condemn “rogue globalist organisations”. But Trump’s ire has been directed most towards the ICC, a separate body which the administration has vowed to dismantle “brick by brick”, rather than the ICJ.

Two other ICJ cases dealing with atrocities in the 1990s Balkan wars fell short of finding states directly responsible for genocide, although Serbia was found legally responsible for failing to prevent or punish genocide committed by Bosnian Serb troops at the Srebrenica massacre.

Other UN tribunals have established genocide in only a handful of cases since the Holocaust, including Cambodia’s Khmer Rouge rule in the 1970s and the 1994 mass killings in Rwanda.

#113. Florida pastor found guilty over role in assassination of Haitian president

Christian Emmanuel Sanon was found guilty of all eight counts against him in a Florida court on Wednesday

A Florida pastor has been found guilty of a number of charges relating to the assassination of the Haitian President Jovenel MoÃ¯se.

Christian Emmanuel Sanon, a dual US-Haitian national, was convicted of all eight charges against him, including conspiracy to kill or kidnap a person. He faces a potential sentence of life in prison.

The 67-year-old was accused of being involved in a plot to replace MoÃ¯se as Haiti's president with himself in 2021, allegedly so he could award lucrative contracts to a Florida-based security outfit once in place.

The murder of MoÃ¯se has left Haiti without a leader for five years. That political vacuum has intensified gang violence in the impoverished Caribbean nation.

A presidential election is scheduled for next year and MoÃ¯se's wife, Martine, who was injured in the attack that killed her husband, announced this week that she intended to stand.

Sanon was found guilty on Wednesday following an eight-week trial in federal court in Miami, according to CBS, the BBC's US partner. His lawyer said he maintains his innocence and that he intends to appeal against the verdict, it reports.

Prosecutors said Sanon had "political aspirations" in Haiti and, in April 2021, met several other co-conspirators to devise a plan to oust MoÃ¯se in an armed raid on the presidential palace in the capital, Port-au-Prince.

Among them were the heads of the companies Counter Terrorist Unit Federal Academy and Counter Terrorist Unit Security - collectively referred to as CTU - who would finance the coup in exchange for government contracts for infrastructure projects, and to provide security forces and equipment.

A few months later, the other conspirators realised that Sanon had "neither the constitutional qualifications nor the popular support" to become Haiti's president and so shifted to another candidate, the US Department of Justice said.

At the same time, the plot had developed from one to forcibly remove MoÃ¯se from office to assassinating him, investigators said.

Dozens of people have been implicated in the conspiracy, which prosecutors said involved co-ordinating funding for and delivering weapons and mercenaries to Haiti to carry out the attack.

On 6 July that year, a group of Colombian mercenaries entered the presidential palace and killed MoÃ¯se. His wife, who was also injured in the attack, said at the time that the assailants had "riddled" her husband with bullets.

Jovenel MoÃ¯se was gunned down by mercenaries in Haiti's presidential palace in 2021

The successful assassination plot raised questions in Haiti about the relative ease with which the mercenaries were able to gain access to the president.

The country - the poorest in the Americas - has been ravaged by gang violence for years, but MoÃ¯se's assassination ushered in a period of worsening instability that emboldened gangs and prompted the deployment of a multinational force.

However, this has struggled to penetrate areas where gangs hold sway and more than 5,700 people have been killed or injured in the year to 11 September, the UN said last month.

Sanon and several other co-conspirators were arrested by Haitian authorities, while three mercenaries were killed in a gun battle with police. Eighteen people were transferred to Florida in September to stand trial over the plot.

Four men, including the heads of the CTU, were convicted of their role earlier this year. Sanon had been scheduled to be tried with them, but his case was delayed due to health reasons, according to CBS.

Sanon was born in Haiti but moved to the US as a teenager. He told the court he had worked in churches in Haitian immigrant communities in several parts of the US including Florida, US media report.

#112. ‘Everyone struggled for this justice’: tears in court as Rohingya vindicated in Myanmar genocide ruling

Relief in the immediate aftermath of a landmark ruling, but what happens next is in the hands of governments around the world

There were tears in the halls of The Hague in the Netherlands and an eruption of applause in the refugee camps of Bangladesh as the scattered communities of Rohingya watched the UN’s top court rule the Myanmar military had committed genocide against them.

For almost a decade, the Rohingya have awaited this moment; recognition from the international court of justice (ICJ) that Myanmar in 2017 intentionally tried to destroy the mostly Muslim minority group through a campaign of mass killings and rape. More than 10,000 Rohingya were killed and 700,000 fled to neighbouring Bangladesh.

By the time they spilled into the court’s halls, the relief was evident. A Rohingya community leader threw a big thumbs up as he left the courtroom and others immediately took out their phones to call family in the refugee camps.

Salma, 30, a Rohingya survivor who lost family members in the 2017 genocide, said: “Everyone worked hard and struggled for this justice, for this cause. When they hear it today, everyone will be happy, thank God. I don’t know how to express it or even what to do with myself.”

Another Rohingya present, who cannot be named because of safety concerns, said they cried as the judge went through each of the rulings.

“During the last few minutes I couldn’t even sit down any more, I had to move to the back because I didn’t know how I’d react,” they said. “I saw some of the other Rohingya around me crying. They were so emotional.

“Now the responsibility is with the international governments. It has been declared genocide now and they have to act.”

In Bangladesh, where most of the Rohingya population have lived – in the world’s largest refugee camp – since being forced to flee in 2017, the verdict was translated into the Rohingya language for the crowds who had gathered to watch on screens specially set up for the purpose.

They immediately leapt up, cheering, applauding and shouting gratitude for the Gambia – the faraway African nation that brought the case to the ICJ on behalf of the stateless minority. Many held posters that read “Thank You Gambia” and “Justice = Safe Return”.

“Since we arrived here, we have been waiting and looking forward to this justice,” said Farida Khatun, 50, who has been a refugee in Bangladesh since 2017, when her village, Tula Toli, was targeted by soldiers. The village in north-western Myanmar, near the Bangladesh border, was the scene of a massacre in August of that year, with reports of mass killings and gang rapes.

She said, however, that she does not expect to be able to return home. Tula Toli was completely destroyed by Myanmar’s forces and no longer exists. “Even though today’s verdict makes us happy, my heart still cannot believe that they will take us back in true peace.”

The case has taken seven years to reach this point, with Rohingya researchers working to collect testimony from survivors. The initial hope for justice had faded during the long wait for a ruling. Many were also left deflated during early hearings when the one-time pro-democracy hero Aung San Suu Kyi defended the Myanmar military at The Hague.

Before today’s ruling, some were optimistic, others were prepared to be disappointed, but all said they knew themselves that they were victims of genocide.

The tension was finally released when the court declared Myanmar was responsible for genocide against the Rohingya, highlighting the use of sexual violence and attacks on children alongside the killing of Rohingya who tried to flee violence, which it said meant the violence amounted to more than ethnic cleansing.

The court ordered Myanmar to provide reparations for the Rohingya and end continuing breaches of the genocide convention.

There is now also an international criminal court investigation into the violence of 2017, looking specifically at the crime of mass deportation, and for which prosecutors in 2024 sought an arrest warrant for Myanmar’s leader, Min Aung Hlaing.

With continuing violence against the Rohingya, the possibility of a return to Myanmar, as happened after previous exoduses to Bangladesh in 1978 and the early 1990s, remains unlikely.

But many hope the ICJ ruling will be the start of a process of ensuring justice and lead to real international pressure to ensure their safety in Myanmar.

“We will be able to return to our homeland – with our rights, with our citizenship, and having received our full security. We will be able to return,” says Salma.

#109. US reacts cautiously to Myanmar genocide ruling as EU praises ‘critical’ role of UN’s top court

Reactions to ICJ ruling underline global divisions over court with China raising concerns about judicial overreach

The US has greeted with caution the world’s highest court’s ruling that Myanmar committed genocide against its Rohingya minority, while China has raised concerns of judicial overreach.

Reactions to Thursday’s historic ruling underlined deep international division over the role of the court and similar institutions. The international court of justice (ICJ) had never previously found a state responsible for genocide and the decision could set a precedent for future genocide claims, notably a case against Israel.

The EU hailed a “landmark” judgment from the ICJ, while the UK reaffirmed “full support” for the court and the international legal order. Australia called on Myanmar to comply fully with the judgment and its obligations under international law.

But China, which has offered rare international support for Myanmar’s military rulers since they seized power five years ago, highlighted “issues such as jurisdiction overreach and expanded interpretation”.

“China … believes that such practices are not conducive to the settlement of disputes,” the foreign ministry spokesperson, Guo Jiakun, told a news briefing when asked about the ICJ ruling. “The international community should make joint efforts in this direction to avoid complicating this issue.”

Western nations cut ties with the unstable and poor south-east Asian country after the 2021 coup that ousted a civilian-led government.

Myanmar’s presidential spokesperson said on Friday that the ruling was “unfair”, noting the judges’ nine to six vote. The country’s military rulers have denied carrying out a genocide, claiming their actions targeted a Rohingya militant group that conducted insurgent attacks.

“When we look at the result, not all 15 judges agreed with the decision,” said Khaing Khaing Soe, speaking at a press conference in the capital, Naypyidaw. “So this was not a unanimous decision … I would say it is not fair.”

The US state department said on Thursday that Washington was examining the judgment, while stressing the need to avoid “politicisation” of the ICJ.

Donald Trump’s administration has taken a dim view of international law, vowing to dismantle the international criminal court (ICC), but has yet to question the legitimacy of the ICJ.

The state department reiterated US support for the people of Myanmar and said it shared “grave concerns over the continued abuses of human rights against Rohingya Muslims and members of other minority groups”, noting that the US was the largest single humanitarian donor to the Rohingya humanitarian crisis response.

In Brussels, the EU said Myanmar must fully implement the ICJ’s ruling “without delay” and that “compliance with ICJ’s decisions is not optional, it is an obligation under international law”.

“The European Union holds the ICJ in the highest regard and remains steadfast in its support for its critical role in the peaceful resolution of disputes and the upholding of the rule of law at international level,” an EU spokesperson said in a statement.

“The Rohingya people have endured immense suffering, including displacement and violence, and their plight must not be forgotten. This landmark ruling underscores the imperative of justice for victims and the necessity of upholding international law.”

The UN secretary general, António Guterres, called the ruling “a demonstration of international justice and a reaffirmation that impunity for such horrific acts must not stand”.

The UK expelled Myanmar’s defence attache and called for measures to “ensure the court’s ruling sends a clear message that such crimes cannot be committed with impunity”.

The ruling caps a lengthy legal campaign since August 2017 when Myanmar’s military launched what it called a “clearance campaign” in Rakhine state in response to an attack by a Rohingya insurgent group. Reports rapidly emerged of mass rapes and killings by security forces, as well as the burning of thousands of homes.

Judges rejected Myanmar’s claim that its actions were primarily intended as counter-terrorism operations, or even that they were an attempt to punish or forcibly displace the population but without committing genocide.

The country’s leader, Min Aung Hlaing, who headed the military during the 2016-17 attacks, has a pending arrest warrant from the ICC – which, unlike the ICJ, prosecutes individuals and not states – on allegations of crimes against humanity.

The former general overthrew the elected government of Aung San Suu Kyi, triggering a civil war that has left tens of thousands dead. He was installed as civilian president in April after elections that were widely discredited.

While the ICJ does not have the power to enforce rulings, its decisions are considered binding, carry diplomatic weight and can shape international action, including sanctions.

Under the 1948 convention, to establish that genocide occurred the court had to find that there was an intent to destroy the Rohingya “in whole or in part”.

About 1.2 million Rohingya refugees now live in Bangladesh, primarily in crowded camps across the Cox’s Bazar district, where they endure harsh conditions marked by fire hazards, infectious diseases, monsoon landslides, inadequate sanitation and strict work restrictions.

Judges at the court based in The Hague have also been asked to rule on other allegations of genocide.

South Africa has accused Israel of breaching the genocide convention during its offensive in Gaza, a claim Israel vehemently denies. That case is years from completion. An interim ICJ judgment in 2024 ordered Israel to ensure its forces do not commit acts of genocide against Palestinians in Gaza.

Ukraine also has a case pending against Russia. Shortly after Moscow’s full-scale invasion in February 2022, Kyiv accused Russia of fabricating genocide claims against Ukraine to justify the attack.

→ Baca artikel asli

#108. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#106. Survivors of Uganda's deadly landslide seek justice at the UN

Bududa has suffered repeated deadly landslides over the years

Survivors of a 2019 landslide in eastern Uganda that killed more than 50 people have taken their fight for relocation and compensation to the United Nations.

The 48 complainants accuse the government of violating Uganda's constitution by failing to put effective disaster management measures in Bududa, a mountainous district prone to landslides.

Their case comes as heavy rains raise the risk of further landslides. They say the government relocation programme ignores their safety, land rights and livelihoods.

The UN Human Rights Committee is considering what is believed to be the first case seeking climate adaptation measures, one that could set a global precedent.

At least 33 of the 53 people who died in the 2019 landslide were children. Years later, survivors say every spell of heavy rain brings back fears of another tragedy.

William Tsama, one of the claimants, lost four members of his family.

"Victims in such situations, ignored by governments, can take them to court to compel them to help their citizens."

Uganda introduced a resettlement plan for landslide-prone areas in 2011, but progress has been limited, with relatively few people relocated.

Last month, it launched a new resettlement programme offering affected households 18m Ugandan shillings ($4,300; Â£3,300) to help them move to safer areas.

But some survivors have questioned whether the amount is enough to cover the cost of relocating and rebuilding their lives.

Under the programme, families can choose between receiving two acres of land, valued at 8m shillings, plus 10m shillings towards building a house, or taking the equivalent land allowance in cash, alongside the same housing grant.

The scheme is expected to benefit 1,410 households identified as living in high-risk areas.

The government has now given residents of designated high-risk areas two months, from November to December, to leave or face forced eviction.

The threat extends beyond landslides. The complainants say changing rainfall patterns and declining soil fertility have also affected farming, leaving many families struggling to secure their livelihoods.

"I want to get a new good place for my people, so even if I die, they will be okay. I am an old man, but I am doing it for my children," Tsama said.

The survivors first launched legal action in 2020 in Uganda, and received support from Ugandan environmental organisation Greenwatch three years later.

They are seeking compensation for lost family members and livelihoods, as well as emergency measures, including accessible early-warning systems in high-risk areas.

Greenwatch Executive Director Samantha Atukunda Kakuru Mwesigwa said the case is about changing how governments prepare and respond to climate-related disasters.

"Justice for claimants is not just about winning in court. It is about forcing government policy to keep pace with a changing climate, and ensuring loss and damage is no longer an afterthought in disaster management," she said.

The threat of climate-related disasters extends across East Africa.

In Kenya, floods and landslides killed at least 181 people between March and May 2024, according to the Kenya Red Cross. In Rwanda, floods and landslides killed 135 people in May 2023.

The dangers are also being felt further afield. In October 2026, a flash flood swept through Nepal's Gorkha district after a landslide blocked a river, washing away homes and infrastructure, and killing over 1,500 people.

"Disasters like the one in Bududa are only getting worse because of climate change â and they are happening all around the world, as we've seen recently and tragically in Nepal," ClientEarth lawyer Vesselina Newman said.

She said poorer countries, which have contributed relatively little to global emissions, are bearing some of the heaviest consequences of climate change and need greater financial support.

"High income countries must provide financial assistance and ensure that sufficient money goes directly to countries like Uganda, so they can roll out life-saving adaptation measures," she said.

The complainants say relocation must involve more than moving people out of danger.

They want the government to protect their livelihoods, respect their land rights, and take account of individual needs.

The case is now before the United Nations Human Rights Committee, which will ask the Ugandan government to respond within six months.

#105. Israel delays building contracts for West Bank settlement after UK sanctions

Attorney general says E1 settlement decision should not be taken by caretaker government led by Netanyahu

Israel has postponed issuing contracts for the construction of a large settlement on the West Bank seen as likely to render a two-state solution unviable, days after the UK imposed sanctions against building firms operating in the area.

Israeli diplomats insisted the move was not in response to the measures from Downing Street. Gali Baharav-Miara, the attorney general, said any decision on settlement expansion should not be taken by the caretaker government led by Benjamin Netanyahu but by whatever new administration was formed.

She ordered the Israel land authority and ministry of housing not to open the tender process. Israeli diplomats suggested the UK foreign secretary, Ed Miliband, had acted prematurely on Monday in sanctioning the eight construction firms. But UK diplomats believe the growing political pressure on Israel over settlements, including from US Democrats, is having an impact.

The deferral puts the E1 settlement expansion in the mix for any coalition negotiations. The leader of the Change bloc, Gadi Eisenkot, seen as Israel’s most likely next prime minister, has been clear in opposing violent settler activity, as has the newly elected Knesset speaker, Matan Kahana, an ally of Eisenkot.

Eisenkot has also opposed the E1 settlement expansion, describing it as a mistake likely to alienate international opinion. He said of the plan: “I recall that Netanyahu himself opposed this for a long time, and due to coalition pressure, he gave in and approved it.”

The EI settlement expansion, due to have been announced on Thursday, has long been seen as qualitatively different since the land – between Jerusalem and Ma’aleh Adumim, a West Bank settlement city of nearly 40,000 residents – bisects the West Bank and cuts it off from East Jerusalem.

Miliband has said it would mark the death knell of a future Palestinian state. The UK, along with a group of European countries, has recently recognised Palestine, and the preservation of a two-state solution has been described as the north star of Labour policy towards the region.

The construction tender was published for more than 3,401 housing units in the area. At the beginning of November, the Israel Defense Forces began demolishing homes in a Bedouin community within the E1 area.

Israel has already enforced the downgrading of the UK consulate in East Jerusalem as a reprisal for Miliband’s decision in September to try to end trade links with the illegal settlements.

#102. Eight injured after driver in Israel repeatedly rams car into Palestinians

Arab MPs accuse politicians of fuelling hostility as footage shows driver accelerating towards Palestinians at four locations in Kafr Qasim

Eight people were injured on Wednesday evening when a driver struck Palestinian residents at four separate locations in Kafr Qasim, a city in central Israel.

Footage shared on social media appears to show the car accelerating towards people, all Palestinians, at several locations in the city, striking them before driving away. Three of those injured were in moderate condition, and several of the victims were women leaving a nearby wedding, the Times of Israel reported.

Haaretz has reported that investigators are examining whether the driver struck people deliberately. Avi Levy, a police district commander, said the incident appeared to be a hit-and-run and that the driver had tested positive for drugs. He said the Shin Bet security service was taking part in the investigation and said: “As of this moment, nothing is coming up. The suspect has no criminal record.”

The incident prompted Arab lawmakers and activists to accuse Israeli politicians of fuelling hostility towards Arab citizens. Benjamin Netanyahu has called Arab parties “terror supporters” and said partnering with them would put Israel’s security at risk. The parties won a record 19 seats in the Knesset in the country’s recent election.

Yousef Jabareen, the chair of the Joint List, said the incident “raises difficult questions about the claim that the driver lost control”. He said: “I’ve been warning for weeks that the incitement against Arab citizens will turn into assaults and even assassinations.”

Said Issa, a community activist in Kafr Qasim, described the incident as an “act of terror”. “Someone who loses control of his vehicle might hit one or two vehicles, but not multiple people in several different places,” Issa said. “The attacker struck at four locations, one after the other.”

In a separate incident in the occupied West Bank, the Palestinian foreign ministry called on Monday for an Israeli settler to be held accountable after eight Palestinians were run over and injured in Tuqu, south-east of Bethlehem. Local sources told the Anadolu news agency that the driver had first struck three young men, then returned and ran over five others who had gathered to help them.

#101. US consortium wins huge Kenya mining deal, beating Chinese rivals

The US is playing catch-up with China's dominance in critical minerals mining and processing across Africa (file photo)

A US-led consortium, Frontier Minerals, has won the tender to develop Kenya's Mrima Hill mining project, beating seven bidders, including companies from China and Australia.

The site is estimated to hold deposits of critical minerals worth more than $60bn (Â£45bn), including niobium, used in electric vehicles, aerospace and defence technology.

Frontier Minerals says its proposal aims to keep more of the economic benefits in Kenya through "processing and refining of minerals in-country".

The deal comes as Washington seeks greater access to Africa's minerals, aiming to strengthen US supply chains and compete with China.

Frontier Minerals brings together three US companies - Bunker Hill Mining Corp, American Resources Corporation and ReElement Technologies Corporation. The consortium combines expertise in mineral resource development, critical materials supply chains and advanced refining technologies.

A central part of its proposal is to develop mineral processing and refining capacity in Kenya. Brad Barnett from Frontier Minerals said the company wanted the country to capture more value from its mineral resources.

Mrima Hill is located in Kwale County, near Kenya's Indian Ocean coastline. The site covers about 390 acres of forest.

The prospect of mining in the area has raised concerns among local residents, who fear the project could damage the forest and threaten their ancestral heritage.

There are also questions over whether communities living near the site will benefit from the investment.

Barnett was keen to reassure Kenyans that they would benefit: "We intend to create lasting opportunities for Kenyan workers, businesses and the wider economy through capacity-building and targeted investment, while prioritising environmental management and performance."

The consortium must still obtain a prospecting licence and meet the necessary regulatory requirements before the project can proceed.

The tender decision has drawn objections from competing bidders.

Reuters news agency reports that two rival companies intend to challenge the selection, with Australia's RareX alleging that Frontier Minerals missed an earlier submission deadline.

Kenya's government has maintained that the process was open and transparent.

The project comes as US strives to secure alternative sources of critical minerals and reduce dependence on China, which dominates the processing of several key minerals.

Washington has been pursuing partnerships with mineral-rich African countries, including the Democratic Republic of Congo and Zambia, as it seeks to strengthen supply chains for industries ranging from electric vehicles to defence technology.

Kenya's mining ministry says the sector is an important part of its plans to diversify the economy beyond traditional sources of revenue such as tourism, agriculture and financial services.

#99. Israeli tanks cross ‘yellow line’ boundary into Hamas-controlled central Gaza

First incursion across boundary since ceasefire deal comes as Eisenkot seeks to form government without Netanyahu

The Israeli military crossed into Hamas-controlled central Gaza on Wednesday in its first ground operation across the “yellow line” boundary since a fragile ceasefire was agreed last October, forcing dozens of displaced Palestinians to flee their tents.

Israeli tanks rolled along the Salah al-Din road, Gaza’s main north-south route, a day after the US-backed Board of Peace, a body overseeing the ceasefire, accused Israel and Hamas of failing to honour their commitments to end hostilities.

An Israeli military spokesperson said troops were carrying out an operation targeting “Hamas infrastructure” west of the yellow line in central Gaza.

The operation lasted about 24 hours and on Thursday the military said it had been “completed”.

The IDF ​has not commented extensively on the operation, which came as the Israeli opposition politician, Gadi Eisenkot, has been seeking to form a government without the caretaker prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, who has built his political reputation on promises to keep Israel safe.

A Gaza security source told AFP there was “intense Israeli artillery shelling” north of Nuseirat refugee camp and reported Israeli military vehicles in central Gaza “amid ongoing gunfire and airstrikes since midday”.

Hamas condemned the Israeli raid and the forced displacement of Palestinians as “a flagrant violation of the ceasefire agreement” and a dangerous escalation. The Israeli military did not immediately respond to Hamas’s statement.

Residents reported hearing tank fire in the area. Palestinian medics said one person was killed in an Israeli airstrike near the site of the raid.

After the statement from the IDF that it had concluded the tank raid to dismantle a suspected Hamas tunnel, the defence ministry’s body in charge of civilian affairs in Palestinian territories, Cogat, said Palestinians would again be free to move in the targeted areas near al-Bureij and Nuseirat refugee camps.

In separate incidents on Wednesday, Palestinian medics said two people were killed by Israeli fire, one in Deir al-Balah and the other in Gaza City. The Israeli military said it was unaware of any incident in Deir al-Balah and had struck a militant in Gaza City.

Israel has stepped up its attacks on Hamas, which it says has been regrouping and rearming. Hamas has accused Israel of trying to derail the US-brokered ceasefire agreed in October 2025.

Israeli strikes have continued since the ceasefire, and Hamas remains armed.

Last week, an Israeli airstrike on Nasser hospital in southern Gaza killed six people, including a surgeon and a disabled man, and wounded 28 others, according to the hospital director.

The body overseeing Donald Trump’s plan to end the war in Gaza has accused Israel and Hamas of failing to honour their commitments to end hostilities, leaving Palestinians in dire conditions and the ceasefire at risk of collapse.

In its second report to the UN security council since the truce began 13 months ago, the Trump-led Board of Peace said the gap between what was agreed and what was happening on the ground remained the biggest threat to the deal. The report, seen by Reuters, warned that “a ceasefire that delivers nothing visible cannot be sustained indefinitely”.

Gaza’s health authorities say at least 1,600 Palestinians have been killed since the ceasefire came into effect. The Israeli military says five of its soldiers have been killed over the same period.

Palestinian authorities say more than 74,000 people have been killed in Gaza since Israel launched its military campaign in response to the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, in which about 1,200 people were killed.

In June, an ⁠independent UN inquiry determined that Israel was continuing to commit genocide by deliberately targeting Palestinian children. A previous report by the commission in September 2025 found that Israel had committed genocide in Gaza, and Israeli officials, including the prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, incited those acts. Netanyahu is separately wanted by the international criminal court (ICC) for war crimes.

#97. Susan Smith denied parole again 32 years after drowning her two sons

Susan Smith's ex-husband makes emotional appeal against granting parole

A South Carolina woman who admitted to drowning her two sons 32 years ago has been denied parole for a second time.

Susan Smith, 55, was convicted of killing her boys by strapping them into their car seats and rolling her vehicle into a lake in 1994.

"I got to my lowest when I let that car go into that lake," Smith said during a hearing on Thursday with the state board that decides whether to release those in prison on parole.

The seven-member board later unanimously turned down her request to be freed. The father of the two boys has vowed to continue to oppose her release, and several people spoke against her in the hearing.

Smith, whose lawyers had argued she suffered a mental breakdown during the crime, can apply to the South Carolina Board of Probation, Parole and Pardon Services for parole every two years.

She told the board that she had changed and matured since killing three-year-old Michael and one-year-old Alex, and that "I am not the same person I was 32 years ago".

She also said she was "very, very sorry" and that she would go back and change the circumstances if she could.

"They were innocent children," Smith said. "They did not deserve to die."

Her children's father, David Smith, attended the hearing so he could advocate for their sons, he said.

The 32 years, he said, do not take away what Smith "deliberately" did.

"I know in my heart granting her parole would be a huge injustice to Michael and Alex," said David Smith, who had pinned a photo of both his sons to his lapel.

Smith was convicted of killing her two sons in 1994

Prosecutors argued during the trial that Smith killed the boys after a man with whom she was having an affair told her that her sons were the reason that the pair did not have a future together.

At Thursday's hearing, Smith said: "This did not happen because of a man. That's just not true."

When the boys first disappeared, Smith said she was carjacked late at night and a man drove away with her car. While she pleaded publicly for their safe return, her sons were at the bottom of a nearby lake.

But ultimately she confessed to the crime.

Defence lawyers said she had wanted to die with her children but left the car at the last minute.

Smith was convicted of murder. Under South Carolina law at the time, she was allowed to request parole every two years after serving 30 years in prison.

In the US, some people convicted of crimes are eligible for parole, which means they can be released from incarceration.

She would have needed two-thirds of the board members present to vote in her favour to be freed.

#96. Myanmar ruling gives new relevance to genocide convention and is ‘full of messages’ for Israel

Hailed as ‘a rare moment of justice’, judges’ decision sets out a roadmap for assessing the question of intent

The international court of justice (ICJ) verdict making Myanmar the first country in history to be found guilty of committing genocide does not just deliver justice for Rohingya victims. It also gives the 1948 genocide convention new relevance in governing the actions of nation states.

In their nine votes to six majority ruling, the panel of judges in The Hague set out a detailed roadmap for how the convention should be applied to real-world situations in which a government is accused of committing genocide against a whole population group, especially when it comes to assessing the question of intent.

The lengthy judgment will have been read particularly carefully by Israel, the only other country currently before the ICJ accused of genocide.

It is also relevant to Germany, which has been brought before the court by Nicaragua on the accusation it has facilitated and failed to prevent genocide in Gaza.

“I think it’s a judgment that brings the genocide convention alive,” said Philippe Sands, a leading human rights lawyer who was part of the Gambian legal team. “It gives the convention practical, real meaning. And if the alternative view had been taken, the convention would essentially become a dead letter.”

Amnesty International’s secretary general, Agnès Callamard, called the ruling “a rare moment of justice for survivors, victims and their families”. But it was also an assertion of humanitarian law in an increasingly lawless world.

“This is a good day for international law. We needed it,” said Dr Iva Vukušić, an assistant professor in international History at Utrecht University and a war crimes expert. “It is really important, clarifying a lot of law, and that’s going to be very helpful going forward.”

There will be a spillover from this ruling to its sister tribunal in The Hague, the international criminal court (ICC), providing a benchmark for when genocide charges could be made against individual perpetrators.

The Trump administration, instinctively hostile to international legal institutions, is also watching critically. The US ambassador to the UN told the ICJ before this judgment not to “lower the legal threshold for proving genocide” lest it be “weaponised” against US and allied soldiers.

The court did not directly lower the standard for demonstrating genocide, and the Gambia – which brought the case to The Hague – did not ask it to.

The judges kept the existing benchmark for judging genocide intent as the “only reasonable inference” of Myanmar’s actions and referred extensively to prior case law. But it went a long way towards clarifying how that standard should be applied in assessing atrocity crimes committed by states.

Before this case, only Serbia had faced the charge of genocide at the ICJ, in cases brought by Croatia and Bosnia and Herzegovina. In the Bosnian ruling in 2007, Belgrade was found to have failed to prevent the 1995 genocide in Srebrenica, but in both cases it was cleared of committing the genocide directly.

The new ICJ judgment finds the Myanmar military directly responsible for genocide against the Rohingya, a Muslim minority targeted in a ferocious counterinsurgency campaign that reached its peak in 2017.

In assessing the Myanmar campaign, the ICJ looked not just at the death toll of about 10,000 in assessing the scale of the atrocities, but at the total Rohingya population targeted in northern Rakhine state, 815,000 people. Significantly, it cast its net wider than just the death toll, considering mental damage done to the survivors, particularly victims of sexual violence, and the impact on reproduction in the Rohingya community.

Most importantly, the ICJ judgment provided a guide to interpreting the standard set by Serbian cases, in which a finding of genocide could be made only if there were not other plausible motives for mass killing.

In this judgment, the court ruled that Myanmar could have had other motives, such as the desire to displace or punish the Rohingya population, but that those motives could exist alongside genocidal intent. The judges said genocidal intent was the “only reasonable inference” from the “totality” of evidence.

In justifying that conclusion the judges pointed to several factors that international law experts say are relevant to the Gaza case, which is now hearing written pleadings and is expected to take at least another three years.

The common factors include the targeting of children, which the court described as striking at the “foundation” of the Rohingya as a group – the ICJ said up to 2,109 Rohingya children under the age of five had been killed, while more than 5,000 Palestinian children under five years old have been killed in the Gaza war.

Another incriminating factor cited by the court was the “bulldozing and large-scale terrain clearance” after the destruction of villages, followed by “rapid construction” including security installations, which has been the hallmark of Israeli army occupation of over 60% of the Gaza Strip.

“It’s full of messages for Israel,” said William Schabas, an international law professor and expert on genocide at Middlesex University. “There are obvious differences, of course, the evidence will be different, but if you exchanged the word Myanmar with Israel, you wouldn’t have to change much in the judgment.”

The fact that there were six dissenting voices on the bench suggested that substantial parts of the majority’s reasoning were not beyond dispute, and lawyers involved in the case cautioned that it did not necessarily mean that the same bench would find Israel guilty of genocide in Gaza. But the same lawyers added that the opposite was almost certainly true. If this case had failed, the case brought by South Africa against Israel would have been all but over.

#93. Venezuela signs military deal with US less than a year after Maduro's removal

Pete Hegseth met Venezuela's interim president at the presidential palace

The United States and Venezuela have re-established military ties less than a year after US forces seized the then-leader of the South American country, NicolÃ¡s Maduro, in a raid on the capital, Caracas.

The interim government led by Delcy RodrÃ­guez, Maduro's former vice-president, signed a military co-operation agreement with US Secretary of Defence Pete Hegseth on Wednesday.

Few details about the deal have been released so far but Hegseth said that Venezuela had "gone from our most aggressive regional threat to a compliant security partner" in less than 12 months.

Interim President RodrÃ­guez said the deal would help confront "common threats".

Military co-operation between the two countries was terminated in 2005 by Hugo ChÃ¡vez, Venezuela's left-wing president at the time.

ChÃ¡vez alleged that US military personnel sent to Venezuela to train their counterparts had made disparaging remarks about his government.

He also accused the US military office located in Fuerte Tiuna, Venezuela's largest military complex, of being "an organ of the CIA... conspiring against the government".

Under NicolÃ¡s Maduro, who succeeded ChÃ¡vez after the latter's death from cancer, relations between the two countries deteriorated further.

They reached rock bottom late last year when the US doubled its reward for information leading to Maduro's capture to $50m (Â£38m) and eventually seized him on 3 January in a military raid.

Maduro and his wife, Cilia Flores, are now in custody in New York on charges of drug-trafficking and conspiracy to commit torture, which they deny.

Hegseth addressed the change in the two countries' relationship since Maduro was seized in his speech at the US embassy in Caracas on Wednesday.

"It's a great honour to be here as the first United States Secretary of War this century to set foot in Venezuela. The weight of this moment does not escape me," he said.

"Just one year ago, my presence here, our presence here would have been deemed impossible, almost unimaginable," he added before recalling a public appearance by then-President Maduro which had angered the Trump administration.

"Exactly one year ago today, the indicted narco-terrorist fugitive NicolÃ¡s Maduro, the man who spent more than a decade handing Venezuela's borders and territory to terrorists from all over the world, stood on a stage, not too far from here, and yelled mockingly: 'Come and get me!'. Well, we answered that call."

Hegseth added that the result of the US military raid on 3 January had been as Maduro predicted, quoting the deposed leader's words: "No war, yes peace."

He described the deal signed by him and Venezuela's defence minister on Wednesday as a "new chapter for the United States, for Venezuela, for the Venezuelan military and for the Western Hemisphere through the Americas Counter Cartel Coalition".

RodrÃ­guez described it as "extraordinarily relevant for our bilateral relations".

"In a matter of months, secretary, we have turned very difficult moments for our people into a space for co-operation and mutual benefit for both of our countries," she said addressing Hegseth during a meeting at the presidential palace.

While no details of the deal have been published, in her speech RodrÃ­guez mentioned the training of Venezuela's armed forces and improving its "operational equipment" and access to new technology "to confront shared threats".

In his speech at the US embassy, Hegseth was more direct about who he considers to be those threats. Addressing "the narco-terrorists who abuse this [Venezuela] as a safe haven", he warned that "we will hunt you down, we will find you, and we will kill you".

From Venezuela, Hegseth travelled on to neighbouring Colombia, whose president, Abelardo de la Espriella, is a close ally of the Trump administration.

Colombia is already part of the US-led Americas Counter Cartel Coalition and in August, Hegseth had floated the possibility of joint US-Colombian military operations against armed groups.

On Wednesday, President de la Espriella took to X to deny a report by the New York Times which had stated that he had rejected carrying out joint operations, external.

In his post, he stressed that any decision would be taken while respecting Colombia's constitution, and the country's laws and sovereignty.

Hegseth reposted the tweet commenting that the US-Colombian partnership "is stronger than ever before".

#92. Iraq university’s removal of male professors teaching women sparks weeks of protests

Al-Qadisiyah University’s move a sign of the country’s slide into a Taliban-like gender segregation, say activists

The removal of male professors teaching female students at a university in southern Iraq has sparked protests and been criticised by activists as a Taliban-like gender segregation policy.

As many as 400 women are estimated to have joined protests over the past two weeks calling for the return of their professors at Al-Qadisiyah University in a move that appears to have surprised its administrators.

The university said the move was necessary to “prevent the mixing” of men and women and had been requested by the families of female students. But students said their families had known since before they enrolled that there were men on the teaching staff and supported their daughters’ protest.

Students said university bosses had mocked their protests and refused to discuss their concerns about the sudden loss of their academic supervisors. On social media, commentators also accused the women of seeking to keep male professors to “establish immoral relationships with them”.

“My classmates and I went to demonstrate, and the university president, Rahi al-Yasiri, blocked our path with his bodyguards, who then began making fun of us. He ordered them to take photographs of our faces, in an attempt to intimidate us and punish us later,” said Huda, 22, one of the female students.

Huda said the students tried to explain how men and women still worked together in society in Iraq. “Aren’t there doctors who treat both men and women? Why then do you prevent male professors from teaching us, too?” In response, Huda said Al-Yasiri replied: “Even if you hit your heads against the wall, I will not bring back the professors.”

The students said a large number of them were tied to graduation projects and research supervised by the professors. They said they had grown accustomed to their professors’ teaching methods, who also knew their students’ strengths and weaknesses, which made replacing them at this stage more complicated.

Female students said they had been told by the university that if they were not happy with the change, they should drop out.

An Iraqi-British feminist researcher, Balsam Mustafa, from the University of Cardiff, said the female students were “treated as if they are minors without the right to object to decisions affecting their academic lives”.

Mustafa said the removal of the male professors was a part of a worrying trend in women’s rights in the country, with “growing discussion about women’s clothing and chastity reflected in the celebration of female students who wear the abaya”.

An Iraqi lawyer and human rights activist, Israa Salman, said the restrictions on women was a “societal catastrophe”.

“Attempts are being made to suppress women’s talents and turn Iraqi society – a society that has produced remarkable minds and expertise, with women playing pioneering roles in many fields – into something resembling Kabul under Taliban rule.

“This contradicts Iraq’s constitution and the international treaties and conventions the country has signed and ratified,” said Salman.

#89. Van Gogh painting sells for record $190.4m after row over its Nazi history

Watch: Moment Van Gogh painting sells for record $190.4m (Â£144.8m)

Auction house Sotheby's has sold a painting by Vincent van Gogh for a record $190.4m (Â£144.8m).

The sale of ChÃ¢taigniers en fleurs ("Chestnut Trees in Blossom") shatters the renowned artist's previous auction record of $117.2 million set in 2022.

The historic sale in New York on Wednesday comes after an eleventh-hour agreement was reached to resolve a dispute regarding claims the art piece was previously sold under duress during the rise of the Nazis.

ChÃ¢taigniers en fleurs was painted in 1890 in the final two months of Van Gogh's life. It is considered one of the most important works of art from that period to remain in private hands, according to Sotheby's.

Sotheby's auction of $180m Van Gogh challenged over claims it was 'sold under duress'

It was not immediately clear who purchased the painting. The BBC has contacted Sotheby's for comment.

The oil painting, which was estimated to carry a value of $180m (Â£136m) prior to the sale, was put up for auction as part of a set called the Blaquier-Arrieta Collection.

Sotheby's announced in the hours before the auction that it had resolved a dispute between the Blaquier-Arrieta family and the heirs of the Fischer family - the prior German owners of the work - regarding the sale.

Representatives acting for heirs of the Fischer family had claimed the painting may have been sold under duress during the rise of the Nazis.

A lawyer for the family's heirs reportedly wrote to Sotheby's on their behalf, telling the auction house that the painting was sold in Amsterdam before World War Two because of financial losses the Fischer family's publishing company suffered under the Nazis. The BBC has contacted the lawyer for comment.

"In recognition of the painting's complex Nazi-era history and the suffering endured by the Fischer family during the Holocaust, the two families have reached a mutually satisfactory resolution and the work will be sold pursuant to their agreement," Sotheby's said on Wednesday.

Sotheby's website details the provenance of the oil painting and how it has researched the artwork changing hands since 1890.

It says Samuel Fischer - a famous German publisher - acquired it in 1909 before it was passed down in his family after his death in 1934.

The painting was deposited with Paul Cassirer in Amsterdam in 1932 or 1933 before it went to Henriette and Marcel Kapferer in Paris in 1936, Sotheby's said.

The Blaquier family acquired it in 1975 and owned it for more than half a century before the latest sale, the auction house said.

Sotheby's staff handle the "ChÃ¢taigniers en fleurs" painting by Vincent van Gogh at an auction preview in Hong Kong last month

#88. Senior Tories urge Badenoch to drop Brexit talk in major strategy shift, sources say

Exclusive: While PM pushes for EU reset, Tory leader is moving away from ‘divisive’ issue that defined party for years

Kemi Badenoch has been advised by her team to limit the amount of time she spends discussing Brexit and Europe, the Guardian has been told, moving the Conservatives away from an issue that defined the party for many years.

While the Tory leader still gives views on Brexit – in an opinion piece in the Daily Mail on Thursday she condemned Andy Burnham’s ongoing reset with the EU as a “backwards” move – sources say it is now seen as a second-order subject.

Instead, Badenoch intends to focus on areas the party believes are more fertile electoral ground, in particular the economy, the cost of social security and defence.

One source said Badenoch had been advised to shift the narrative away from Brexit by Stephen Gilbert, a former Conservative official who is now a peer and who joined the Tory leader’s team as a senior political adviser a year ago.

Although another party source said Gilbert’s advice was “more nuanced” than this, Badenoch’s recent run of speeches and interventions appears to demonstrate the change.

In eight sets of prime minister’s questions since Burnham entered No 10 over the summer, Badenoch and her deputy, Alex Burghart, who took one session when Burnham was away, have focused on Europe just once, on 28 October, when she challenged the PM on whether or not he wanted a new Brexit referendum.

Every other outing has focused on various aspects of the welfare state, the cost of living, or defence, also including subjects such as the budget, debt and whether or not the UK should drill for more North Sea oil and gas.

“The way I see it, this is less of a conscious attempt to sideline Europe than it is a way to talk more about the subjects we think are our strong points, like the economy and defence,” one senior Conservative said.

One Tory shadow minister said the wider party was firmly behind the idea: “Europe does still come up from time to time, but as a former remainer even I think we should move on. I’m not sure what is likely to change by us talking about it all the time.”

Another shadow minister said: “We don’t want to be talking about Brexit for multiple reasons – including the simple one that it was so awfully divisive.”

Badenoch does still at times defend Brexit to selected audiences, for example in Thursday’s Daily Mail piece, where she disputed Burnham’s charge that it had hampered the UK’s economic growth, saying the prime minister “wants to reopen arguments from 2016 because he has no answers for the world as it is”.

At other times her language on links with the EU has been more nuanced than the period when the Conservatives liked to disparage the continent as economically moribund and less relevant than more distant economies.

Asked about Burnham’s attempt to renegotiate the post-Brexit trade agreement during a press conference on defence earlier this month, Badenoch said: “They say they want to end red tape on food checks – that’s a good thing”, while then predicting this would not actually happen.

Badenoch also said she accepted that the deal with the EU could and should be updated: “The free trade agreement that we have with the EU, like many others, is one that needs constant negotiating. So I want to see Labour negotiating and improving on deals with all countries. That’s what you do when you have your own trade policy.”

A Conservative spokesperson said: “As Kemi has said, the last thing the country needs is to be dragged back into a debate on Brexit. That absolutely does not mean Kemi and the Conservatives won’t discuss the EU, but we will be very clear that Andy Burnham is only trying to reopen old wounds on Brexit because he doesn’t have anything to say about the challenges facing the country.

“Bringing down the welfare bill, funding defence, cutting taxes to promote growth can all be done outside the EU. Burnham’s reset is one big distraction and we will never shy away from saying so.”
