#54. Fiona the hippo dies after swallowing a hat at Cincinnati Zoo

From premature baby to social media star - Famed Cincinnati Zoo hippo Fiona dies

The Cincinnati Zoo in Ohio says its beloved hippopotamus Fiona has died just days after swallowing a knitted hat that slipped into her enclosure.

"It is with extreme sadness that we share with you the devastating news that Fiona did not make it after multiple attempts from our amazing vets and keeper team to help her through what ended up being a medical emergency," the zoo said on Thursday afternoon.

Fiona, who became a social media star after her premature birth in 2017, swallowed the hat about 10 days ago and required emergency care.

The vet staff "tried everything they could to help her" but "it was ultimately too much to overcome", the zoo said.

Fiona initially seemed fine after swallowing the hat more than a week ago

"We are in shock at the moment," Cincinnati Zoo director Dave Jenike said in a statement. "We did not expect this outcome. She fought until she couldn't anymore."

Michelle Curley, a spokesperson for the zoo, had said that the hat got into Fiona's enclosure by accident, after a visitor tossed it in the air and it caught on a ceiling fan that flung it through a small opening.

"We probably couldn't make [it] happen again if we tried," Curley said.

While she seemed fine at first, her appetite started to decrease a few days later, and the zoo staff tried to encourage Fiona to eat to help push the hat through her digestive system.

An ultrasound before her death showed the hat passing through Fiona's small intestine. A zookeeper said surgery was not an option for full-grown hippos and "our best chance for success with Fiona would be if she could pass this on her own".

The zoo also tried a different approach to attempt to get the hat to pass by encouraging Fiona to stand on a vibration plate platform borrowed from a local horse farm.

"Our animal care team was heroic in their efforts to save her. They did everything possible, but her body couldn't fight anymore," Jenike said.

Fiona was born premature at the Cincinnati Zoo in 2017 and at first struggled to survive. At birth, she weighed 13 kg (29 lbs), which the zoo said was about half the previous lowest recorded birth weight for her species.

Cincinnati's mayor said Fiona brought a smile to many faces

Her death is also very premature, with hippos normally living up to 50 years in human care, according to the zoo.

"Although this was not an intentional incident, of which, regrettably, there are many, Fiona's painful death is a tragic reminder that everyone should behave carefully and respectfully around animals," said Debbie Metzler, the managing director of captive wildlife for the animal rights group People for the Ethical Treatment of Animals (Peta).

"Peta expects that the zoo will review its protocols to help prevent anything like this from happening again, and we urge every visitor to secure their belongings and never toss objects near an animal's enclosure."

As well as being famous online, Fiona's story inspired an ice cream flavour and a character in Cincinnati's ballet.

Cincinnati Mayor Aftab Pureval said Fiona "was a symbol of perseverance and joy" in the city.

"She brought smiles to so many faces, from kids to parents to visitors from around the world," he wrote on X. "Our city is heartbroken, but we couldn't be more grateful that she was part of our lives and our community as a whole."

Fiona the hippo undergoing care after swallowing a hat at Cincinnati Zoo

#53. Baby born with heart abnormalities ‘didn’t have a chance’ after non-medical free birth, NSW inquest hears

Inquest into the deaths of four babies after free and home births hears routine checks and follow-up may have saved their lives

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Four babies who died in New South Wales – three after free births and one after a home birth – might have survived if they had had routine checks and follow-up , a coronial inquest has heard.

Baby DH, Baby D, Baby R, and Baby B died in the state’s northern rivers region between 2022 and 2024.

The inquest in Lismore has heard women chose free or home births for reasons including traumatic experiences, a lack of trust in clinicians, and a lack of services.

Baby DH died about eight hours after he was born in a free birth – with a doula but no medical professionals present. The court earlier heard he turned purple, was struggling to breathe and had became floppy.

His parents gave evidence they were worried but the doula kept reassuring them their baby was OK, then left.

The doula denied many of their claims, and denied that she gave them any clinical advice.

Expert neonatologist and associate professor Nicholas Evans said on Friday that Baby DH’s complex heart abnormalities “would have been readily detectable” in a routine pregnancy scan after which he would have been referred to a paediatric cardiologist.

It was a “missed opportunity”, he told the court.

Paediatric cardiologist Dr Paul Brooks said it was “highly likely” all the abnormalities would have been identified in a routine scan before birth.

Even if the extent of the abnormalities wasn’t clear, he told the court, the scan would have shown the baby needed to be born at a major hospital with cardiac surgery capabilities.

“The site of [Baby DH’s] birth meant that he didn’t have a chance.”

Baby D’s mother did not know she was having twins, and at first thought she’d delivered Baby D’s placenta, only to realise it was a stillborn second twin.

Experts at the inquest agreed it was impossible to know exactly why Baby D died. They discussed the possibility it was because self-described birth keeper Sita Tara Kali did not clamp the umbilical cord before cutting it, but Evans told the court that it was ultimately most likely from respiratory distress because of his compromised heart function.

Baby D might have survived if someone who could do advanced resuscitation had arrived in under 10 minutes, he said, but the “root cause” was that it was not known the mother was carrying twins before the births.

“If [twins] had been diagnosed, at 18 weeks, as it would usually in pregnancy, there would have been very close monitoring.”

Baby R likely died of “severe post-maturity” after being born at 43 weeks and one day in a free birth. The standard is to induce birth or perform a caesarean section at 42 weeks, as the ageing placenta puts the baby at risk.

The mother had been for an ultrasound at 12 weeks, which had put the date of birth at 28 July, 2020.

But the mother distrusted hospitals and trusted Kali, her birth keeper, the court previously heard.

Kali told her to calculate her due date from the date of conception, an unreliable measure that put the date of birth in mid-August.

That recalculation, Evans wrote in his report to the court, was the “root cause” of the issue.

“The standard practice now is that you don’t allow a pregnancy to go beyond 42 completed weeks,” Evans said on Friday.

Expert midwives earlier told the court they didn’t have statistics for babies born beyond 43 weeks because it was so rare.

A 19 August ultrasound showed the pregnancy had become a medical emergency – but Kali advised her to “go home and rest”. It is “more likely than not” that the baby would have survived had she gone to hospital after the ultrasound, Evans said.

Baby B’s mother was warned multiple times she needed to go to hospital to give birth to her twins, but she was determined to have a home birth. Rather than let her have a free birth, four midwives assisted her and the head midwife repeatedly counselled her about the risks.

The mother declined ultrasounds as well as a test for early onset group B streptococcus (GBS) – an organism present in 15 to 20% of women that infects the babies of about 2% of carriers.

Baby B likely died of GBS, pneumonia and sepsis, Evans told the court on Friday, while his twin fell ill but survived. Many infected babies survive in hospital thanks to antibiotics, he added.

#50. US army warns detonation of unstable explosives will create toxic plume

The US Army says that detonating the unstable materials in place is the safest option to dispose of the degrading substance

The US Army is set to detonate five tonnes of degraded explosive material in Middletown, Iowa, and have urged locals to vacate the area or stay indoors for their safety.

The detonations will result in an air plume that could send toxic dust, containing lead particles, over eastern Iowa and into neighbouring Illinois, with the potential for contaminants to settle on farmland, playgrounds and other surfaces, the army said.

The two controlled explosions will be powerful enough that they could damage buildings and shatter windows nearby.

On Thursday, environmental groups sued in an effort to stop the detonations, arguing the army failed to consider safer alternatives or give adequate notice.

The detonation was originally scheduled for noon local time (1800 BST) on Friday at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant (IAAAP) in Middletown - a community of about 360 people about four hours west of Chicago, Illinois.

The plant produces medium- and large-calibre munitions for the US Department of Defense.

On Thursday evening, Acting Secretary of the Army Adam Telle delayed the detonation to Monday, saying that "public safety is the Army's top priority".

The planned detonation will be "the latest possible moment prior to a forecasted temperature drop, which would risk further destabilization of the material", he said on X.

The army said that the detonation is part of "a planned and controlled safety operation" to dispose of degraded lead azide. It added that the detonation has been permitted by the US Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) under conservation and recovery laws.

Residents have been asked to leave their homes for about six hours - between 08:00 and 14:00 local time - while the operation takes place and the dust settles. The army said that "all humans" should avoid outdoor activities within affected areas.

Residents in Middletown will hear a loud noise, feel vibrations from two consecutive, rapid detonations and will see a plume of dust, the advisory said.

According to Iowa Public Radio, external, the IAAAP found earlier this year that the lead azide - a highly volatile compound used as a detonator - stored in underground concrete tanks is degrading, becoming too unstable to safely move.

Military and environmental officials determined that detonating it in place is the safest way to eliminate the explosive safety hazard.

The EPA, which granted an emergency permit to detonate 68 drums of the material, said it did so because the explosion risk "presented an imminent and substantial threat to human health and the environment".

Justine Barati, a public affairs specialist at Army Sustainment Command, said that the substance has been stored at the plant for more than 20 years in earthen-covered containers.

It sits in a bath of ethanol and water to keep the material stable, but a routine check earlier this year found that some of the water had evaporated, Barati told the Associated Press.

The army said the lead azide barrels will be detonated inside earth-covered magazines made of concrete, and surrounded with barriers full of sand. The soil above will be saturated with water in an effort to reduce the lead in the air.

On Thursday, two environmental groups sued to block the detonation, challenging the validity of the EPA's emergency permit, according to the BBC's US media partner CBS News.

"The EPA itself admits that there is no safe level of lead exposure for humans," said Michael Schmidt, General Counsel at the Iowa Environmental Council.

"When our federal government makes decisions that result in a potentially toxic plume of lead dust that could cover much of southeastern Iowa, Iowans deserve to know that every available alternative was considered before choosing this method of disposal."

#49. Vape users hospitalised after losing consciousness, prompting public health warning in New South Wales

Testing confirms presence of etomidate, a strong and fast-acting sedative used as a general anaesthetic overseas but not registered for medical use in Australia

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Three people who lost consciousness and were hospitalised in New South Wales after using certain vapes have tested positive for the unregulated anaesthetic agent etomidate, health officials said.

NSW health on Friday issued a public warning about vape liquids containing etomidate, a strong and fast-acting sedative used as a general anaesthetic overseas but not registered for medical use in Australia.

The three people who were hospitalised had used non-nicotine refillable vapes, the state health department said. Testing of their blood and urine confirmed the presence of etomidate.

The department would not confirm how old the three patients were, where they were hospitalised, or their condition on Friday.

The discovery of etomidate in vapes in NSW comes about four months after Victorian health officials announced they had found the powerful anaesthetic agent in an illicit vape sold in Melbourne.

On Friday, NSW health warned vapes sold as space pods, space oil, kpods, piu piu vapes, or zombie vapes may contain etomidate, or other substances, including ketamine, THC and potent opioids.

The medical director of the NSW poisons information centre, Dr Darren Roberts, said etomidate’s effects were unpredictable when used in a vape.

Roberts said etomidate could cause confusion, drowsiness, slow breathing, vomiting, muscle spasms or movements that were hard to control, and people who took it could become unresponsive.

These effects could be life threatening, especially if used with other drugs that could cause sedation, such as alcohol, opioids, benzodiazepines, and GHB, Roberts said.

He said etomidate could also cause long-term harm when used regularly, including the disruption of hormones produced by the adrenal gland.

These hormones are important in regulating stress responses and need to be replaced if they aren’t produced naturally, he said.

“The contents found in non-nicotine vapes vary,” Roberts said. “You can’t assume what’s inside.”

He urged anyone considering using a vape to ask themselves what could be in it.

“You won’t get into trouble for seeking medical care,” he said. If you feel unwell, or if your friend feels unwell, after using a vape, do something about it.”

NSW health officials said it recommended people administer Naloxone to anyone who became responsive after using any drug, in case they had ingested opioids.

Naloxone is an opioid overdose treatment, which is free at pharmacies and does not require a prescription.

Naloxone won’t reverse the effects of etomidate, as the sedative does not belong to the opioid class of drugs, but it is still safe to give, health officials said.

The officials said anyone who witnessed someone experiencing unexpected symptoms after using drugs should call triple zero immediately or seek urgent medical attention.

#46. As the Coalition stares down One Nation in NSW, a new threat is rising: the teals

The opposition’s struggle is creating an opportunity for progressive independents who are boosted by shrewd political operator Chris Minns

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

The New South Wales Coalition is facing a renewed threat to its heartland seats on Sydney’s north shore.

This time, the threat is from independent candidates – and they’re backed by the same community activists who put a swathe of teal independents into federal parliament.

The challenge, which has so far remained under the radar, comes as the NSW Coalition tries to shore up its right flank against a surging One Nation before the March state election. This has resulted in the Coalition lurching to the right on issues ranging from gun control to law and order, and, in the case of the NSW Nationals, to abandon climate targets altogether.

As former opposition leader Mark Speakman resigned from the Liberals on Thursday – joining an exodus of MPs announcing they wouldn’t contest their seats in March – he warned his party against becoming “One Nation lite”.

But the move to the right has also created an opportunity for progressive independents running in urban seats usually held by Liberal moderates, with several candidates about to launch their campaigns.

This weekend, Amelia Loye, a management consultant who specialises in stakeholder and community engagement, will announce she is running in the seat of Willoughby, on Sydney’s north shore. She will attempt to oust Liberal MP Tim James, who holds the seat on a 2.6% margin.

Loye is backed by community group Willoughby Votes, which came close to securing an independent in the 2023 election with 47% of the two-party preferred vote.

“Willoughby has already shown it is ready for an independent,” she said. “Nearly half of voters backed one last time. I want to earn their support, and the support of those still deciding.”

She joins high-profile candidate Elouise Massa, a small business owner, who became a household name after campaigning for Northern Beaches hospital to return to public hands after her toddler’s death in emergency.

Massa will run in the Sydney seat of Manly against Liberal Adelaide Cuneo, who is seeking to replace the retiring MP, James Griffin.

The independent deputy mayor of Mosman, Michael Randall, announced last weekend he will run in North Shore as a community independent. Several of the people behind former independent federal MP Kylea Tink’s campaign are supporting him.

Monica Wulff, another independent, has announced a run in Davidson.

Voters on the northern beaches have already sent two independents to Macquarie street – and both have a good chance of being returned.

At the federal level, teal independents Sophie Scamps, Zali Steggall and Nicolette Boele hold McKellar, Warringah and Bradfield respectively. Tink held North Sydney for one term before the seat was abolished.

The NSW Liberal party is not giving up on the northern beaches and the north shore, despite the trend towards independents.

“We’ll keep making our case directly to voters, including why we oppose Labor’s gun buyback that targets law-abiding owners rather than criminals and terrorists,” a Liberal spokesperson said.

This time, however, the independents have a boost in the form of NSW Labor premier, Chris Minns.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

During the final weeks of parliament before the NSW election, Minns joked that independent Jacqui Scruby, who won Pittwater at a byelection, should be known as “the member for Mona Vale road” for her tenacity in seeking an upgrade. It’s now being fast-tracked by the Minns government.

Minns has also appeared with Massa, and praised her for her advocacy.

But Minns, a shrewd political operator, has not fallen in love with independents – he’s just hedging his bets.

A year ago, with the Liberals languishing in the polls, Labor could have expected to increase its majority in 2027. But with One Nation storming to a primary vote as high as 30% in some polls, that prospect has dimmed.

In the current parliament, Labor has support of three independents – Greg Piper, Joe McGirr and Alex Greenwich – to ensure confidence and supply for its minority government. An expanded crossbench would give more options and provide a buffer for Labor losses to One Nation in western Sydney, the Hunter and possibly the Central Coast.

If all independents were successful – and that is a big if – almost the entire north shore region would go to state independents, except the seat of Wahroonga.

Most of the commentary about the 2027 election has focused on the likely Armageddon facing the Nationals, who could lose as many eight or nine of their 11 seats in the regions.

But a teal wave also remains a threat to the Liberal party, especially as the Coalition try to position themselves to fight off One Nation in the regions with policies that don’t sit well with urban voters.

One Nation could also prove helpful to independents if it makes good on its promise to run candidates in every seat. The party is more likely to shave votes from the Liberal primary vote, making it easier for the independents to overtake the Liberal on preferences from Labor and the Greens.

NSW’s optional preferential voting makes it hard to predict how strong flows will be – and it depends what parties advocate on how to votes. But in 2023 only 41% of One Nation voters nominated preferences, compared to 70% of Green voters and 49% of Labor voters.

The opposition leader, Kellie Sloane, has not ruled out either a preference deal or a post-election arrangement with One Nation, describing such options as “hypothetical”. But if they do end up preferencing One Nation, it may alienate moderate Liberals and ultimately work against them.

Disclosure: Anne Davies worked as a media and policy adviser to the independent federal MP Sophie Scamps from October 2023 to October 2024.

#43. New page in history? Xi, Trump and Putin summit was more of a footnote

Far from the grand ambitions declared in Yalta in 1945, Shenzhen felt like a meeting in search of an agenda

It is arguably true, given it was the first such meeting of the leaders of China, Russia and the US, that Thursday’s gathering in Shenzhen deserved the description of “historic”.

But in terms of tangible outcomes, it is unlikely to warrant the “new page in the history of diplomacy” that Vladimir Putin claimed had been written, and will instead be consigned to a smallish footnote in any future volume of the 21st century.

It’s true that the meeting could yet be an icebreaker and the start of a permanent new dialogue. It’s also true that each leader can return home with something from the 90 minutes they spent together.

Despite falling asleep during the main Apec summit, Donald Trump had a chance to show his proximity to two authoritarian leaders he admires. With Ukraine having destroyed Russia’s bridge into Crimea, the US president greeted Putin as not just a friend but almost a wartime ally.

The Russian president, although occasionally pushed to one side by protocol during the early photo opportunities, will be able to show back home that it is Moscow, and not his adversary Europe, let alone Ukraine, that commands the respect and attention of Trump and Xi Jinping. As such, the summit was a free gift to Putin, and underlined that Trump was no longer the leader of the free world, as the former British foreign secretary Jeremy Hunt commented.

From Putin’s perspective, a meeting in Berlin of the coalition of the willing was a sideshow. Europe’s attempts to isolate Russia had failed and the Asia-Pacific region had become the centre of the global economy, Putin boasted. His relationship with Trump, he said, despite the difficulties, was founded on “similar civilisational values”.

For Xi, it was a prestigious chance to play host and implicitly demonstrate that the era of American hegemony is over. No longer the sole arbiter in world affairs, the US, the consumer economy, must share the stage with China, the manufacturing giant, and Russia, the energy bank.

The trio’s brief joint statement covered four pretty disparate topics: boosting energy supply, reforming the United Nations, limiting the powers of the international criminal court (ICC) and a tentative commitment to discuss nuclear disarmament and non-proliferation.

For grandeur at a time of various global crises, these ambitions hardly matched Yalta in 1945 or even the declaration after the Tehran summit in 1943, when the leaders of the UK, US and Russia sat on the veranda of the British embassy and pledged that their countries would work together after the war to build a “world family of democratic nations” free of tyranny and oppression. Winston Churchill declaimed in Tehran: “In our hands we have the future of mankind.” Joseph Stalin went further. “History has spoiled us,” he said. “She has given us very great power and very great opportunities.”

Shenzhen, by contrast, felt like a meeting in search of an agenda. The vacuum was filled with platitudinous commitments to “maintain strategic stability and international security”, a task that – looking around the world – none of those in attendance are currently pursuing with great effect.

Perhaps technology, multipolarity and their own divisions mean even they do not have “very great power”. Certainly there were no accompanying plans for how to reform the UN, let alone boost energy production or show respect for international law – a subject Trump’s current favoured successor, Marco Rubio, had just disparaged in a speech as a plaything of the global elite.

It was the summit’s chief instigator, Putin, who was the most eager of the three to set the meeting in a historic context, describing China, Russia and the US as the victors of the second world war, in a deliberate slight to Britain. A pledge to “preserve and strengthen the system of international law” seemingly referred to ensuring certain world leaders were freed from the prospect of the ICC serving them with arrest warrants.

By coincidence, the other world court, the international court of justice (ICJ), found a state – Myanmar – guilty of genocide for the first time on the same day. The Shenzen statement did not name the ICJ as a target, beyond an oblique reference to the ICC and “other similar institutions”. Doubtless that is only because ICJ rulings can be dismissed with near impunity.

Xi, nervous that China’s status as protector of the global south might be damaged by being involved in a global superpower carve-up, stuck to a familiar script: “History tells us that when major powers are at peace, the world is secure. When they clash, there is chaos.”

It may be that behind the scenes, or at other events, serious progress is being made on how China might act as a guarantor of a peace deal with Iran, or on who should lead the UN through its coming phase of reform, or even on how Putin might bring himself to seek peace in Ukraine in a way that protects Ukrainian sovereignty.

For the moment, though, it looks as if what little history was made in Shenzhen was made for exclusively the wrong reasons.

#41. Trump accused of falling asleep during Xi Jinping speech at Apec summit in China

US president appeared to doze off while Chinese leader made speech, but White House insisted he was awake

Donald Trump was, again, accused of falling asleep during a televised event, this time during the speech of the Chinese president, Xi Jinping, at the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (Apec) summit in China.

The US president appeared to close his eyes for roughly 10 seconds on Thursday. As his eyes closed, Trump’s head tilted downward, and his hands remained clasped in front of him.

Several prominent Trump critics on social media alleged that the US president was asleep, including Republicans Against Trump, California governor Gavin Newsom’s press office and conservative lawyer George Conway.

White House officials denied the allegations, claiming the 80-year-old US president was looking down at papers in front of him.

“Fake News! President Trump was looking at papers in front of him while the speaker made remarks. You can clearly see his eyes move back and forth,” Steven Cheung, the White House director of communications, wrote on X in response to a Newsweek article.

“This is why the people despise media so much. They constantly lie,” Cheung added.

Trump often faces accusations of falling asleep during televised events such as Oval Office press conferences, cabinet meetings and sporting events.

Occasionally, while listening to other people speak, the US president will close his eyes for several seconds, appearing as though he is falling asleep. Despite footage of the instances going viral, the White House and Trump allies have insisted the US president does not fall asleep during meetings.

Administration officials have excused Trump’s apparent drowsiness as blinking or an example of the president listening intently. They consistently claim Trump “doesn’t sleep”.

Marco Rubio, the US secretary of state, testified to lawmakers earlier this year that he had “never seen” Trump fall asleep.

“On the contrary, the guy doesn’t sleep, which is a big problem because he calls me at two in the morning,” Rubio said in June.

Last year, JD Vance said that Trump hardly ever sleeps, even while traveling overnight.

“If we go on a 20-hour trip somewhere, he doesn’t sleep the entire time. And of course, if he’s not sleeping, if he’s working, he expects everybody else to be working too,” Vance told Fox News.

Trump became the oldest US president ever inaugurated when he was sworn in nearly two years ago. Despite his age, Trump often claims he is in near-perfect health, often bragging about his “perfect” cognitive test results and insisting he has a lot of energy.

#40. Myanmar committed genocide against Rohingya minority, top UN court rules

Rohingya genocide: What was said in court and what happens now?

Myanmar has been found guilty of committing genocide against its Rohingya minority by the International Court of Justice (ICJ).

The verdict marks the first time a state has ever been found to have committed genocide by an international court.

Yuji Iwasawa, president of the UN's top court, said Myanmar had the "intention to destroy" a substantial part of the Rohingya population and engaged in "extensive destruction of evidence" to hide its crimes.

A military crackdown in 2017 killed thousands of Rohingya and forced more than 700,000 to flee Buddhist-majority Myanmar, formerly known as Burma. Myanmar denied the claims against it, saying its forces had responded to attacks by armed groups.

Bowen: Myanmar genocide verdict is historic and could have far-reaching implications

The court found on Thursday that "genocidal intent is the only reasonable inference that can be drawn from the evidence" it had seen.

The verdict was reached by a majority of nine votes to six among a panel of judges from around the world. The ICJ also found in favour by majority that Myanmar had violated several other parts of the 1948 Genocide Convention.

Rohingyas at the Cox's Bazar camp in Bangladesh, where around a million refugees now reside, watched the verdict live

The case brought by The Gambia centred around so-called "clearance operations" initiated in 2017 in Rakhine State, where many of the Rohingya Muslim minority resided.

After fighters from the Arakan Rohingya Salvation Army (Arsa) launched deadly attacks on more than 30 police posts there, Myanmar troops, backed by local Buddhist mobs, launched a wave of violence, prompting a mass exodus of Rohingya.

Refugees recounted entire villages being burned down and the mass killing of civilians, while numerous survivors and eyewitnesses provided testimony that the Myanmar military had also raped and abused Rohingya women and girls.

The ICJ determined that around 10,000 Rohingya were "violently killed" during the clearance operation, while more than 700,000 are thought to have fled to Bangladesh by foot or by sea. The court found that approximately 815,000 had been targeted in some way.

Watch: Genocide judgment will 'mean a great deal' for Rohingya community

Iwasawa said Myanmar's military engaged in "indiscriminate" killings, the execution of men, "widespread" sexual violence, "extreme violence" against children and the deliberate burning of Rohingya homes.

He said the military had also attacked Rohingya as they were fleeing to Bangladesh.

Myanmar, he added, had engaged in "extensive destruction of evidence" by burning or disposing of bodies, burned and bulldozed sites that were cleared and failed to prosecute those who had perpetrated the acts of violence.

While the ICJ recognised the role Arsa played, it found there had been "overlap" between an "anti-Rohingya hate speech campaign" - which portrayed the Rohingya as "sub-human" and "a violent threat" - and the clearance operations that made violence against the population more likely.

Myanmar consistently denied genocide against the Rohingya, saying its operations had targeted militant or insurgent threats in response to the Arsa attacks.

In January, its representative Ko Ko Hlaing told the ICJ that the country "was not obliged to remain idle and allow terrorists to have free reign". He said he had "no comment" following Thursday's verdict.

The ICJ also looked at the wider treatment of Rohingya leading up to the clearance operation and found that policies like limiting married couples to two children were discriminatory and aimed to erode their rights.

It said Myanmar had an obligation to pay proportionate reparations, but said an exact figure could be determined at a later date.

The court's decision was welcomed by Rohingya communities and human rights groups.

"This is like formal recognition of our suffering," says Mohammed Nowkim, a Rohingya who was driven out of Rakhine in 2017 and now lives in Canada. "The Rohingya have been waiting for justice for decades."

Human Rights Watch said the ruling was "long-awaited recognition of the suffering the Rohingya have endured".

Meanwhile, UN human rights chief Volker TÃ¼rk said the "suffering, misery and humiliation" inflicted on the Rohingya people was a "stain on the conscience of humanity". He added that the court's decision "must now translate into real action".

To implicate the Myanmar government in genocide, the court had to determine not just that terrible acts had been committed, but that there was an intent on the part of officials or arms of the state to partially or completely destroy the group.

Thursday's ruling is without precedent. Myanmar now carries the stain of being the first nation to have been found to have violated the 1948 Genocide Convention.

The ICJ has only twice before ruled on claims of state-backed genocide in the years since the Genocide Convention was signed in the aftermath of the Holocaust, in both cases finding there was insufficient evidence to attribute the actions to the state.

Dozens of individuals have been found guilty of genocide, but finding their actions were at the direct behest of the state requires a higher evidential bar to prove genocide, the gravest crime under international law.

Rohingya refugees were seen reacting as the ICJ verdict was broadcast on a big screen in Cox's Bazar, Bangladesh

Philippe Sands KC, a top human rights lawyer who helped bring the case, described it as an "historic judgment".

He told BBC Radio 4's World at One programme that it was "right up there with the great moments of international justice: Nuremberg, Pinochet, and others", referring to the trials of Nazi leaders following World War Two and that of the Chilean dictator, respectively.

However, the ICJ is not a criminal court and its powers of enforcement are limited. It cannot send military officials to prison, while it relies on the UN Security Council and political pressure to impose its rulings.

The verdict will have implications for other genocide cases pending with the ICJ, though, including one brought by South Africa against Israel over its war in Gaza.

Sands said that pending case had been the "elephant in the room" during the Myanmar hearings.

He added that, while ruling against Myanmar did not mean the court would find similarly with Gaza, if it had not found Myanmar responsible, the case against Israel "would, in effect, be over". Israel says the case against it is "wholly unfounded".

ICJ president Yuji Iwasawa said Myanmar had the "intention to destroy" a substantial part of the Rohingya population

Antonia Mulvey, executive director of Legal Action Worldwide, told the BBC that the court had not accepted Myanmar's counter-terrorism defence and had found for the first time that rape and sexual violence was an act of genocide, positions it would bear in mind for future cases.

A US State Department spokesperson said "the proper interpretation" of the Genocide Convention was of "paramount importance", as was "preventing politicisation" of the International Criminal Court.

Noting that the US was the "leading donor for the Rohingya refugee humanitarian response", they said they shared the "grave concerns over the continued abuses of human rights" against Rohingyas and other minorities, adding: "We are studying the decision to fully assess its implications."

Following Thursday's ruling, UK Foreign Secretary Ed Miliband said the government was ordering Myanmar's defence attachÃ© be expelled and that he was summoning its charge d'affaires.

He pledged to set out "further action in the coming days to ensure those responsible are held to account" after a cross-party group of MPs called for the government to resume sanctions against Myanmar's military.

#37. China, Russia and US agree peace declaration with no mention of Ukraine and Iran wars

Xi, Putin and Trump outline priorities that include UN reform, boosting oil supplies and ‘protection of diplomatic immunity’

China, Russia and the US say they have agreed a peace statement after an unprecedented trilateral summit, but the declaration made no mention of the wars in Ukraine and Iran caused by two of the participants.

The declaration on Thursday also outlined a set of priorities that included changing the UN, boosting oil supplies and what it said was the protection of diplomatic immunity.

The statement affirmed that “a nuclear war cannot be won and must not be fought”, but stopped short of suggesting the beginnings of a framework for denuclearisation.

The meeting between Xi Jinping, Vladimir Putin and Donald Trump has dominated the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (Apec) summit this week, with China, the host, promising it would be “groundbreaking”.

“We are really making history,” Trump told Xi and Putin at the start of the meeting. “No peace was ever forged by declining opportunities for engagement.”

Putin told a press conference later that the three men had had a “free discussion” and now had a “mutual understanding”.

Some had hopes that the talks, which lasted about one hour and 20 minutes, could be used to discuss reductions in arms between the world’s three largest nuclear powers. Beijing has previously rebuffed Trump’s advances to join such talks, pointing to the larger US and Russian stockpiles. On Thursday, though, Xi called for nuclear weapons to “be completely prohibited and thoroughly destroyed over time to make the world free of [them]”.

The language of the declaration around nuclear war is the same as that previously issued by the five permanent members of the UN security council, which includes France and the UK.

“The real test will be whether the three countries follow through on their stated commitment to discuss nuclear risk reduction and preventing nuclear proliferation,” said Patricia Kim, an expert on Chinese foreign policy at the Brookings Institution, a US thinktank. Even the suggestion that China would engage was significant, said Yun Sun, a senior fellow at the Stimson Center.

Analysts had said that the meeting’s primary significance was in its optics. “Overall, very little of substance seems to have been accomplished … This summit appears to have been all about the symbolism and prestige value,” said Prof William Hurst, from the University of Cambridge’s Centre for Geopolitics. Putin pushed back at the suggestion the summit had just been a photo-op. “It is a serious collaborative work,” he said.

In the declaration, the men “committed themselves to maintaining strategic stability and international security”.

First among their concerns was “promoting UN reform so that it may respond more effectively to global challenges”. Sun said this was a favourite topic of China’s and showed that Beijing was “flexing its diplomatic muscles”. She said: “It is able to set the agenda and the deliverables according to its priorities … and make other great powers accept them.”

The declaration also agreed that “the international criminal court [ICC] and other similar institutions must not abuse their jurisdiction”. Putin is subject to an ICC arrest warrant and so the clause added to the perception that he has done well from the summit. Since the 2022 invasion of Ukraine, the Russian president has been treated as an international pariah by Kyiv’s allies and he has not been to an Apec summit in years.

Hurst said: “This was no Yalta moment, but it was certainly a solid public demonstration of Russia’s (and Putin’s) continuing relevance and power. It’s yet another example of Putin playing a poor hand to maximum effect.”

Xi, his status as a global power broker now assured, began the morning by addressing Apec leaders, reiterating the themes of trade openness and innovation he has emphasised throughout the summit. However, Apec affairs appeared to take a back seat for most of the day, with business dominating the discussions. For all the camaraderie on display, relations are complex. Russia is under US sanctions over the Ukraine war, while China and the US spent much of the last year engaged in an escalating tit-for-tat trade war before Trump and Xi called a truce.

Trump and Putin earlier held a separate meeting – their first since August 2025 – where the US president called his Russian counterpart his “friend”.

European allies meeting in Berlin to show their support for Ukraine expressed indignation at the cosy optics. “Mr Putin is not our friend and ⁠will never be,” the Polish prime minister, Donald Tusk, said in a rejoinder to Trump. The Danish prime minister, Mette Frederiksen, said she found it “very incomprehensible” that a US ​president would want to sit down with Putin.

The peace declaration in Shenzhen said the three countries planned “to increase the production and supply of refined petroleum products for the benefit of all nations”. That was important, said Shan Guo from Hutong Research, a Shanghai-based advisory firm, but “now let’s see if the US will lift sanctions”.

Trump and Xi then held their third bilateral of this year. Xi said the US-China relationship was “generally stable and positive”, according to state media. But he cautioned Washington over Taiwan, saying the US “must keep doing the right thing to avoid major setbacks in US-China relations”.

Another watched-for topic lacking detailed findings was that of AI. Xi said the two countries should “enhance communication and coordination to guard against security risks … and ensure that AI remains under human control at all times”.

Ultimately, the lack of breakthroughs might suit China, which has no appetite to move beyond the role of convener, said Kim. “Beijing has little interest in taking on the diplomatic risks of brokering an actual peace agreement in Ukraine or resolving the conflict with Iran. That would entangle China in conflicts it would rather avoid,” she said.

#35. Trump, Putin and Xi release 'peace declaration' after historic meeting

Watch: What did Trump, Xi and Putin get out of their historic meeting?

US President Donald Trump and his Russian and Chinese counterparts Vladimir Putin and Xi Jinping have issued a "joint declaration of peace" following historic trilateral talks.

The three leaders met for 90 minutes in China's south-eastern city of Shenzhen, on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (APEC) summit.

While the presidents have previously had one-on-one meetings, these are their first trilateral talks.

In the declaration, they said "nuclear war cannot be won and must never be fought" and that the countries planned to increase the production and supply of petroleum. They also said they were "committed to reform the UN" to respond more effectively to global challenges.

Thursday's statement also flagged the issue of immunity enjoyed by state officials and said "they agreed that the International Criminal Court and other similar institutions must not abuse their jurisdiction".

Trump praised the trilateral meeting calling it "a great and highly consequential trip", in a social media post while flying back to Washington on Air Force One.

In the same post, the president announced he would host a news conference to talk about the trip on Friday at the White House.

While in China, Trump met Putin ahead of the trilateral, in their first meeting since August 2025, where the US president greeted his Russian counterpart as his "friend".

He also spoke with Xi in a separate conversation, describing the pair's relationship as "very special".

Beyond the warmth and shared niceties, however, the three men had different goals and motivations for the sit-down.

Analysis: Trilateral summit high on spectacle, short on substance

This is a remarkable diplomatic moment for China's Xi - but it comes with pitfalls

The opening remarks of the trilateral gave some indication about what was to be discussed during the closed-door meeting.

Before giving the floor to his fellow leaders, Xi said that "positive interaction among major powers is not a matter of choice, but a mandatory requirement".

Putin followed this by highlighting a shared interest in ending the Ukraine war and said the conversation would also touch on food and energy security.

China has officially remained neutral since Putin launched a full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022, rejecting accusations from Kyiv and its allies that it has been tacitly supporting Moscow, including by exporting dual-use technology used by the Russian military.

Beijing has also repeatedly called for negotiations and a political settlement while avoiding condemnation of Russia's aggression.

Trump greets 'my friend' Putin ahead of meeting with Xi

Meanwhile, Trump closed by saying the presidents of three of the world's largest countries have a "special responsibility" to maintain productive dialogue.

"Too often past leaders have avoided talking to nations with whom they had disagreements," he said, adding: "We feel the opposite."

Trump referenced the three countries' nuclear capabilities and said they are "uniquely positioned to discuss how to reduce the number of nuclear weapons on Earth".

It is "vital" that they co-operate to "combat the threat of nuclear proliferation in other countries around the world", he said, adding: "That's what we're doing in Iran."

"We're not going to let Iran have a nuclear weapon".

Trump then turned to Ukraine and said he and Putin have had a "long discussion" on the conflict there already.

He added that Putin "and I hope the other side, would like to see an end to that war".

Putin addressed the media following the trilateral meeting and described it as an "important chapter in the history of diplomacy".

He said Ukraine and Europe were not yet ready to make compromises to resolve the conflict in Ukraine - but he said Russia had invited EU representatives to Moscow to better understand Europe's position.

As the talks were under way in China, and even as Putin was holding his press conference claiming to be ready for peace, Ukraine's capital Kyiv was under attack from Russian drones.

Putin has repeatedly rejected all ceasefire offers from Ukraine, insisting on his maximalist demands which Kyiv and its European allies say are tantamount to de facto capitulation of Ukraine.

In Shenzhen, the Kremlin leader also vowed a "far more painful" retaliation in response to Ukrainian attacks on the bridge connecting Russia to Ukraine's occupied peninsula of Crimea. A missile and drone attack on Tuesday destroyed its central road section.

There was no mention from Putin of almost five years of Russian attacks on power plants, bridges and residential buildings in Ukraine.

The Russian president also directly addressed comparisons between his meeting with Trump and Xi and the 1945 Yalta Conference, where the leaders of the Soviet Union, Britain and the US met towards the end of World War Two.

"Nobody divided up the world at Yalta. It simply codified the realities that had emerged after WW2.

"And when it comes to today's conflicts, the realities on the ground will have to be accepted."

The three presidents spoke behind closed doors at the Kylin Villa, the exclusive resort in Shenzhen

The Kremlin released a video showing Putin leaving the summit venue and saying goodbye to Xi.

"Thank you for organising this," Putin said. "I don't think anyone even expected everything to go so well."

Meanwhile, China's president described his meeting with Putin and Trump as "positive, constructive, and highly productive", according to the country's state broadcaster CCTV.

It added that Xi recalled "the history of fighting side-by-side during WW2", as the three leaders emphasised the importance of "cherishing the hard-won peace".

Thursday's trilateral meeting was reportedly the idea of Putin.

Some Russian newspapers called it a "diplomatic victory" as after Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine many Western leaders were trying to isolate him.

The summit also marked the first time since Russia's war in Ukraine began that Putin took part in an international gathering attended by leaders from countries including Canada, Japan and Australia.

Russian 'nuclear briefcase' spotted at APEC summit

US lawmakers responded with frustration to Trump's warmth towards America's geopolitical adversaries.

"I was encouraged by some of the commitments that came out of the President's meetings in Asia, particularly the recognition that nuclear war must never be fought," Republican Senator John Curtis said in an emailed statement to the BBC.

But Curtis also said he was concerned about Trump's approach to the foreign leaders, adding that "Vladimir Putin and Xi Jinping are not our friends".

"We should not treat them as though they share our values or interests," Curtis, who sits on the Senate foreign relations committee, added.

"Doing so risks undermining our national security, weakening trust with our allies, and sending the wrong message about where America stands."

Jeanne Shaheen, a ranking Democratic member on the same committee, chose harsher words in her comment.

"President Trump's 'friend' is a murderous war criminal," she wrote on X.

With additional reporting from Brandon Drenon

#33. California investigates Nvidia over whether chip exports ended up in China

State attorney says scandal ‘raises bright red flags’ and aims to find out if company misled investors

California has served an investigative subpoena on Nvidia after a scandal that prompted the departure of co-founder and longtime chief executive Jensen Huang.

Rob Bonta, the US state’s attorney general, issued the subpoena as part of an investigation into whether exports of the California-based company’s advanced semiconductor chips ended up in China, contravening US restrictions.

“The conduct of Nvidia and its former chief executive raises bright red flags, including about the ways in which the company may have misled investors,” said Bonta.

“Nvidia is in a messy, transnational situation but the underlying principle of our corporate securities law is clear: companies cannot mislead their investors.”

The subpoena, issued on Thursday, followed Huang’s resignation a week ago after it emerged he had multiple affairs, including one with Alice Huang, a suspected Chinese spy.

Alice Huang, who is not related, is associated with Singapore-based Megaspeed, which has bought nearly $5bn (£3.8bn) of Nvidia chips and hardware in recent years, and is being investigated for possibly diverting advanced restricted chips to China.

Megaspeed has said it is a “legitimate and lawful” business, and that Huang left the company late last year.

Bonta said the investigation would also look at whether the former Nvidia chief’s actions resulted in confidential information being passed to China.

“My office is investigating whether Nvidia or Jensen Huang’s actions resulted in China obtaining advanced chips or confidential information,” he said. “And if Nvidia misled investors and the public about this risk, in violation of the law.”

Nvidia’s shares are down by a fifth since Huang admitted the extramarital affair, hitting a one-year low at the close of trading on Thursday, and wiping almost $1tn off its market value.

Earlier this week, Harvey Jones, Nvidia’s chair, said the company had no intention of reinstating Huang after the former CEO said he would be “honoured” to return. Elon Musk and a host of tech bosses had campaigned for Nvidia to bring him back.

Huang is due to testify before several congressional committees over the potential breach of national security on 1 December.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

Shares in rival chipmaker AMD closed down almost 8% on Thursday after the publication of reports connected to short seller Hunterbrook warning about its links to China and the “circular financing” of customers.

The company, which has been targeted amid the Nvidia scandal, is looking at a further fall of more than 5% in pre-market trading.

Hunterbrook Media published allegations on the use of AMD’s software by a Chinese server maker blacklisted by the US. Sister publication, The Bear Cave newsletter, alleged that AMD’s use of datacentre lease guarantees and backstops for unsold cloud computing capacity could leave it exposed to a pullback in AI spending.

AMD said the reports’ claims “misrepresent AMD’s business practices and relationships”.

“AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations,” the company said. “We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity.”

AMD’s share price has more than doubled so far this year, giving the company a market capitalisation of more than $800m.

#31. Anthropic becomes one of world's most valuable public companies in stock market debut

Watch: How did Anthropic's stock market debut unfold?

Anthropic has become one of the most valuable public companies in the world after its shares began trading on the stock market, taking its valuation to roughly $1.76 trillion (Â£1.34 trillion).

The artificial intelligence (AI) company's Thursday debut on the Nasdaq stock exchange was a crucial test for the industry, given the huge amount of money being poured into advancing the technology.

Anthropic's shares flew past its allocation price of $83 each, showing there was plenty of excitement around AI.

Yet the company, which is behind AI chatbot Claude, will remain under intense scrutiny in the months ahead to answer the key question surrounding AI: is it all worth it?

In just a few hours of trading, Anthropic's stock seemed to show that investors remain uncertain of where exactly AI is heading. The share price rose immediately to $111.53, sending the company's market value to almost $2 trillion, before falling by the end of the day as the shares closed at $97.17.

Some investors said Anthropic's debut seemed to be a success, as shares rose roughly 17% during the day, but the broader tech market did not perform as well.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index closed down nearly 2.3%. Stocks fell in the largest tech firms, including Google's parent company Alphabet, which was down by 2.9%, while Amazon lost almost 3.9%.

The buildout happening around this era of AI is among the most expensive endeavours in the history of technology, with more than $1 trillion being spent in the last year alone by a handful of major tech companies.

The speed at which investment has grown has led to ongoing concerns of a financial bubble.

"Bubbles don't burst on the day of the largest deal," Richard Whittle, an economist with the University of Salford said. "They burst later, if the positive future becomes suspect."

Anthropic has made one of the fastest trips from start up to IPO in history

While the basics of the technology have been around for decades, the more recent advent of more powerful computers and related components have allowed the processing of more data than ever.

The result has been AI chatbots that simulate human speech and operate tasks like software coding, digital image generation, and searching the internet more quickly.

It represents a new way of using computers that has led to the rapid rise of Anthropic, which started just five years ago and is best known as the maker of the popular AI chatbot Claude.

As the first purely AI company to go public, Anthropic will almost certainly see significant swings in price on the stock market going forward, according to Ben Barringer, who leads technology research at Quilter Cheviot.

The "initial exuberance" will wear off, Barringer said, pointing to what happened with SpaceX earlier this year.

That company, led by Elon Musk, saw shares jump by about 10% on the first day of trading, taking the company to a record-breaking valuation of $2.2 trillion.

Within a month shares had fallen below their debut price, and have mostly fallen further since.

Most tech companies see their shares fall after hitting the stock market, often after reporting quarterly results for the first time.

But many such companies, including Meta, Amazon and Google, have gone on to become the largest and most profitable corporations in the world.

With Anthropic, investors are not only looking at a notable tech company with a major valuation, and a potentially big future in business, but a company that is the first of its kind on the stock market.

"There's no obvious peer to compare it to," Neil Wilson, an investor strategist at Saxo UK, said.

Simon Jack: Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

Daniela Amodei: Anthropic and the price of AI

So, as individual investors and even researchers and investors at large funds ask themselves if Anthropic is really worth more than $1.7 trillion, or even more, there will be few answers available.

"We'll have a stab at it," Wilson said. "I don't think anyone really knows."

A few things will likely impact the company's success on the stock market in the near-term.

One of those things is the prospect of new laws governing the use of AI tools. In the US, where Anthropic is based, Democrats will be in many more positions of power come 2027, and several politicians have already said they will be looking to ensure AI tools are safe and have some guardrails attached.

Such regulation is currently unlikely at the federal level. US President Donald Trump on Thursday spoke about AI during the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in China, alongside Chinese and Russian counterparts, Xi Jinping and Vladimir Putin.

Trump reiterated his position against "any form of robust regulation" around the technology, adding, "we won't allow that to happen, frankly."

The same day, however, a bipartisan group of US lawmakers formed the House AI Safety and Innovation Caucus, which will work specifically on AI regulation and safety considerations.

A significant driver of this kind of scrutiny is Anthropic itself. Company executives, including co-founder and CEO Dario Amodei, have been outspoken in their purported fears that future AI tools will become so capable they will outsmart humans, to the point where extinction is possible.

Anthropic has always put itself forward in public as an AI company that takes the safety of its technology very seriously.

Daniela Amodei, Anthropic's president and sister of Dario Amodei, boasted on Thursday that the company "had an AI safety team before we launched our first product".

Despite the company's claims that the technology it is building at a rapid pace could be dangerous, Anthropic has made one of the quickest trips from start-up to IPO in history.

Anthropic raises $81bn in record-breaking stock market debut

#30. Anthropic shares slump on second day of trading as stock markets reel from AI sell-off

Asian markets record their worst week since 2020 as investors question huge sums being poured into AI build-out

Anthropic’s shares slumped at the start of trading on Friday, as its record-breaking IPO failed to stem a sell-off in AI-linked companies.

Shares in the frontier AI company, which floated on Thursday, fell by over 9% at the start of trading on Friday. They dropped to $88.17, towards the $83 level at which the company sold shares in its IPO this week.

Anthropic’s first two days as a listed company have been volatile. Its shares jumped by 17% on Thursday, after briefly surging by almost 40% when trading in the frontier AI firm began.

Global markets slumped on Friday too; Asian stock markets recorded their worst week since 2020, as shares in big chip companies fell. In Taiwan, stocks dropped 6.7% to hit their lowest point since mid-April, with the chip maker TSMC down about 6%.

The scale of the decline in Taiwan prompted the chair of its stock exchange to pledge to ensure the stable operation ⁠of capital markets, and reassure investors they should remain confident in the fundamentals of Taiwanese companies.

In Japan, the Nikkei index fell 5%, while South Korea’s tech-heavy Kospi index lost 5.5%. Shares in the tech investor SoftBank had their worst day in more than two years, down 14.5% in Tokyo, amid worries around its aggressive bets on AI.

European stock markets were heading for their worst week since March, with the pan-European Stoxx 600 dropping 1.7%. London’s blue-chip stock index hit a five-month low.

The sell-off followed losses on Wall Street on Thursday, where the main indices all fell by more than 2% on a day dominated by Anthropic’s IPO.

Chris Beauchamp, the chief market analyst at the investing and trading platform IG, said Anthropic could be “draining market liquidity”, as investors sell shares in other companies to raise funds to pay for stock in the newly floated AI firm.

“The slide from yesterday’s highs sends a message that there might be more at work here than investors realise, and while brokers are scrambling to buy more, that liquidity has to come from somewhere, and the other tech names may be the first ones to feel the pressure,” Beauchamp said.

Anthropic’s flotation has failed to ease worries around the AI trade, as investors increasingly question whether companies can deliver the returns necessary to justify the billions of dollars being poured into the AI build out.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

Daniela Hathorn, a senior market analyst at Capital.com, said: “The IPO demonstrated that demand for AI exposure remains strong, but it has not been enough to overcome concerns about elevated valuations, profitability and the sustainability of investment spending.

“The broader market mood has shifted from buying the dip towards questioning whether the correction has further to run. The weakness in equal-weighted indices suggests that selling pressure is no longer confined to a handful of technology giants, while Europe’s recent outperformance has also faded.”

Nvidia shares fell by 3%, after California served the chip company with a subpoena as part of an investigation into whether its advanced semiconductors ended up in China.

Britain’s FTSE 100 share index has dropped by 4.8% this week, and is heading for its biggest weekly fall since the start of the Iran war.

Fears that US central bankers may raise interest rates to fight inflation have lifted the dollar to a 19-month high, and pushed down the gold price – with gold on track for its worst week since March.

#28. Europe ready for talks with Russia on ending war in Ukraine, leaders say

European leaders have said they are ready for negotiations with Russia on ending the war in Ukraine, in a strong show of support for Kyiv.

"Europe has a clear and consistent and united negotiating position," UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham said, while German Chancellor Friedrich Merz stressed that Moscow's "maximalist demands are not realistic".

They were speaking at Berlin's summit of Ukraine's allies known as the Coalition of the Willing.

Donald Trump's son-in-law Jared Kushner, one of the two US envoys who were in Berlin, said the US president wanted to make a deal with Moscow "with Europe by his side". Russia's Vladimir Putin has so far rejected any European role in the US-led peace talks.

Trump had earlier held historic trilateral talks in Shenzhen, south-eastern China, with Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping and later dialled into the Berlin summit from Air Force One.

In a lengthy press conference following his talks with Trump and Xi, Putin addressed the question of multilateral peace talks, saying the Americans were "insistently" asking Russia to allow the Europeans to take part.

Putin claimed Russia was not against the idea but said Moscow wanted to clarify "what Europe will bring to these talks".

"That is why, through diplomatic channels, we have invited the Europeans to come to Moscow in order to determine their positions. And after that, we will decide in what format we will continue the dialogue with Ukraine."

But Putin also said that "every day" Russia heard that European leaders were talking about war coming in 2030. "But we are not planning to attack anyone... Is that normal? I do not think so," he said. Still, Putin added, Russia understood that "discussing issues linked to security in Europe is pointless without the Europeans".

Jared Kushner told reporters that the US president had come away from Shenzhen believing that "peace is achievable".

Striking an upbeat note, Kushner said that the Russians were willing to trust the instinct of the US envoys "on our behalf" that the Europeans should be at the table and that Moscow was now "comfortable" with Ukraine having security guarantees based on the idea enshrined in Nato's Article Five that requires allies to come to a country's defence if it comes under attack.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky, who also was at the summit in Germany's capital, has long sought a guarantee equivalent to Nato's collective defence clause, and there has been little indication that Putin would accept that kind of condition.

Russia rejects outright the idea of Ukrainian membership of Nato, but reports say US negotiators have mulled the idea of giving Kyiv Article Five-style guarantees without being part of the Western defensive alliance if it agrees to painful territorial concessions.

Zelensky and the three European leaders â Merz, Burnham and French President Emmanuel Macron â were not as upbeat as Kushner, and the Ukrainian president made clear that the Russian president had spurned every proposal so far: "Putin has always said no."

The German chancellor was equally sceptical, although he said with US involvement the chance of peace was increasing. Europe was willing to enter "serious discussions on European security" with Russia, he explained, "as long as it's matched with a serious discussion and only first a peace agreement with Ukraine".

"These talks have to begin, and we will accept if conditions are acceptable for Europe and Ukraine," said Merz.

He also said any attempts to divide Europe into spheres of influence run by major powers was unacceptable, amid concerns on the continent that this issue may have been on the agenda at the trilateral talks in Shenzhen.

Analysis: Trilateral summit high on spectacle, short on substance

"A 'Yalta format' will not bring peace," Merz said, referring to the post-World War Two agreement between the US, the USSR and Britain in 1945.

He also sought to downplay the Shenzhen summit as above all "a photo opportunity."

"The US understands that there can be no peace without us. We, in turn, know that with the United States by our side, the prospects for peace increase," Merz added.

Polish Prime Minister Donald Tusk also took exception to Trump's description of Putin as a "friend" at the Shenzhen summit, saying: "Mr Putin is not our friend and will never be."

Meanwhile, Macron urged Ukraine's allies to do the "maximum" to deliver the much-needed air defence systems Kyiv needed to repel deadly Russian attacks.

The French leader also urged the allies to "reopen the question" of Ukraine's membership in Nato.

There have been mixed messages from Moscow on European participation to the talks. Last week, following a visit to Moscow, the US envoys, Steve Witkoff and Kushner, appeared certain that Europe would soon have a seat at the negotiating table.

But the Russians were more circumspect and this week Putin's aide Yury Ushakov said a "great deal" would have to be done before the format could be expanded, suggesting that several more rounds of discussions would have to be held before any four-sided talks could take place.

Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022.

Several buildings were damaged in the latest Russian attack on Ukraine's capital Kyiv

While Russia has engaged with several rounds of talks over the last year, little in Moscow's rhetoric or actions suggests that the Kremlin is considering scaling down its war on Ukraine.

It continues to pound Kyiv's energy infrastructure to cripple it as winter takes hold, increasing numbers of civilian casualties are being recorded across the country, and Kremlin officials have not budged from Russia's demands for Ukrainian sovereign territory.

As delegations were meeting in China and Germany on Thursday, several buildings in Kyiv's historic centre came under attack from drones. One person was killed in a strike on a residential building in the Podilskyi neighbourhood, while the Dutch and Austrian embassies as well as Saint Volodymyr's Cathedral reported damage.

And Putin also vowed that Moscow's response to a Ukrainian strike on the Crimean Bridge linking Russia with occupied Crimea would be "considerably more painful" than what Kyiv had achieved.

He also said he had discussed a "possible ceasefire in a number of areas" but had decided against it after the attack on the bridge.

Still, Putin claimed to be "interested" in ending the war and was ready to make compromises, while Ukraine and Europe were not.

"I assure you, we have no desire at all to continue this armed conflict," Putin said, despite the fact that Russia has in recent months intensified its attacks on Ukraine's critical infrastructure as cold winter approaches.

Kyiv and its European allies say that â in the absence of any major success on the vast front line â the Kremlin leader is seeking to break Ukrainian resistance by leaving millions of civilians without electricity and water supply.

UK to provide Ukraine with 1,000 long-range strike drones

#27. Caribbean nations fear economic impact of shuttering citizenship program after EU threats

St Kitts and Nevis to wind down citizenship-by-investment program after EU warns of end to visa-free access

The decision by St Kitts and Nevis to end a lucrative programme offering citizenship to wealthy foreigners in exchange for investment threatens its economy and ability to support people in need, the premier of the island of Nevis has warned.

On Friday, Terrance Drew, the prime minister of the twin-island Caribbean nation, announced he would wind down the citizenship-by-investment (CBI) programme, after the European Union threatened to end visa-free access for St Kitts and Nevis.

Mark Brantley, the premier of Nevis, which has autonomous self-governance in some aspects, said he understood that the “country was given very little choice”, but did not “support the winding down of the program”.

The 42-year-old CBI programme, which St Kitts and Nevis pioneered, allows wealthy foreigners to obtain citizenship and ultimately a passport by investing in infrastructure and other development projects. It has brought billions of dollars into the country and in some years accounted for the largest share of government revenue.

In June, the European Commission wrote to the five Caribbean nations that currently operate CBI programmes: St Kitts and Nevis, Dominica, Antigua and Barbuda, Grenada and St Lucia. It warned that countries may lose visa-free access to its 29-country Schengen area if they did not phase out CBI.

In Friday’s statement, Drew blamed concerns over the programme on mismanagement by previous administrations, including years of underselling to undercut other countries, a controversial prison project, weak escrow protections and investment sums not being properly paid into the citizenship unit.

He said his government had led major reforms to the Caribbean’s programmes with international partners, including the US treasury, but argued that continuing the programme risked visa-free travel to parts of Europe and access to international banking. That could disrupt services allowing visitors to use overseas cards and families abroad to send money home.

Brantley, however, argued it was unfair for the EU to force St Kitts and Nevis to make a move without offering assistance to navigate the transition to other industries.

“I have not yet seen from the EU any corresponding suggestions of assistance. And we have to realise that these citizenship-by-investment programs, not only in St Kitts and Nevis, but … smaller countries of the Caribbean, have contributed significantly to our gross domestic product and also to ensuring that our people … can get adequate access to water and to education, to healthcare and the like,” he said.

Brantley described the 1 June 2028 deadline the EU gave the country to phase out the lucrative programme as “frankly ludicrous”.

In his statement, Drew said the CBI programme “did a lot of good, but it also created a danger”.

“When one source pays for half or more of everything, you are at its mercy. If it stops, everything stops. That is putting all of your eggs in one basket,” he added.

The prime minister said the government aimed to diversify its economy by strengthening tourism, yachting, and digital and creative industries, among others. He added that the sale of clean, geothermal energy was “at the heart of it” with drilling and exploration already planned.

“I will not pretend that the road is easy. Less CBI money means we must spend wisely and plan carefully. We will. Our plan is to make this change by growing our economy. Raising taxes is not part of that plan,” Drew said.

Antigua and Barbuda has also expressed willingness to end the programme, though it is trying to negotiate a longer transition period. The five Caribbean governments have taken differing positions on the EU demand. According to local media, Grenada has indicated it wants its programme to continue until 2032, while Dominica and St Lucia have responded to Brussels without publicly committing to ending their programmes.

Jermine Abel in Basseterre contributed reporting
